            \draw (A) to [bend left=15] (C);
            \draw (A) to [bend right=15] (C);
            \draw (A) to [bend left=15] (D);
            \draw (A) to [bend right=15] (D);
            \draw (A) to [bend left=12] (B);
            \draw (B) -- (C);
            \draw (B) -- (D);
            \node[below] at (1.8,-1.75) {\textbf{Abstract graph $G$}};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}
\end{topic}

\begin{definition}
    An Eulerian path is a walk which contains each edge of a graph exactly once.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    An Eulerian cycle is a closed Eulerian path. A graph is called Eulerian if it contains an Eulerian cycle.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    Let $G = (V,E)$ be a graph with $|E| \ge 1$. If every vertex has degree at least $2$ (i.e., $\deg(v) \ge 2$ for all $v \in V$), then $G$ contains a cycle.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Take a maximal path $P = (v_0, v_1, \ldots, v_k)$ in $G$ (so no edge extends $P$ at either end). Since $|E| \ge 1$, we have $k \ge 1$. The vertex $v_k$ has $\deg(v_k) \ge 2$, so $v_k$ has a neighbor other than $v_{k-1}$. Any such neighbor must lie on $P$: if $v_k$ were adjacent to some $u \notin \{v_0,\ldots,v_{k-1}\}$, then $P$ could be extended to $v_0 \cdots v_k u$, contradicting maximality. So $v_k$ is adjacent to $v_i$ for some $i$ with $0 \le i \le k-2$. Then $(v_i, v_{i+1}, \ldots, v_k, v_i)$ is a cycle in $G$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    A finite connected graph is Eulerian if and only if every vertex has even degree.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \emph{Necessity ($\Rightarrow$).} Let $C = (x_0, x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ be an Eulerian cycle with $x_0 = x_n$. Each edge is used exactly once. For any vertex $y \in V$, each time $C$ passes through $y$ it uses one edge to enter and one to leave; so the number of edges incident to $y$ used by $C$ is twice the number of times $y$ appears in $(x_1,\ldots,x_{n-1})$. The start/end vertex $x_0 = x_n$ is left once and entered once, contributing $2$ more. Thus $\deg(y)$ is even for all $y$.

    \emph{Sufficiency ($\Leftarrow$).} (Hierholzer, 1873.) We use induction on $|E|$. If $|E|=0$, then $G$ is a single vertex and the trivial closed walk is an Eulerian cycle. Suppose $|E| \ge 1$. Since $G$ is connected and every vertex has even degree, $\delta(G) \ge 2$, so $G$ contains a cycle by the lemma above. Start at any vertex $x$ and take a maximal walk that does not repeat any edge. Whenever the walk enters a vertex other than $x$, it has used an odd number of edges at that vertex so far, so an odd number (hence at least one) remains; thus the walk cannot get stuck except at $x$. So the walk returns to $x$, forming a closed trail $C_1$ with edge set $E_1 \subseteq E$.

    Remove $E_1$ from $G$. In $G' = (V, E \setminus E_1)$, every vertex still has even degree (each vertex on $C_1$ lost 0 or 2 incident edges). So every non-trivial component of $G'$ has an Eulerian cycle by the induction hypothesis. If $E_1 = E$, then $C_1$ is an Eulerian cycle. Otherwise, pick a vertex $v$ that lies on $C_1$ and belongs to a component $H$ of $G'$ with at least one edge. That component has an Eulerian cycle $C_2$ (starting and ending at $v$). Splice $C_2$ into $C_1$ at $v$: traverse $C_1$ until $v$, then traverse all of $C_2$, then continue $C_1$ from $v$. Repeating this for every such component yields an Eulerian cycle of $G$. The algorithm runs in $O(|E|)$ time.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    Consider the graph below. On the left is the original connected graph; on the right we redraw the three edge-disjoint cycles that appear in the proof of Hierholzer's theorem.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.0, every node/.style={font=\small}, line cap=round]
            % Original graph
            \coordinate (p) at (0,2.6);
            \coordinate (u) at (-1.2,1.4);
            \coordinate (v) at (1.2,1.4);
            \coordinate (x) at (-1.2,0);
            \coordinate (y) at (1.2,0);
            \coordinate (q) at (0,-1.2);

            \draw (p) -- (u) -- (v) -- (p);
            \draw (u) -- (x) -- (y) -- (v);
            \draw (u) -- (y);
            \draw (x) -- (v);
            \draw (x) -- (q) -- (y);

            \node[vertex, label=above:$p$] at (p) {};
            \node[vertex, label=left:$u$] at (u) {};
            \node[vertex, label=right:$v$] at (v) {};
            \node[vertex, label=left:$x$] at (x) {};
            \node[vertex, label=right:$y$] at (y) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:$q$] at (q) {};
            \node at (0,-1.9) {original graph};

            % Top triangle
            \coordinate (p1) at (5.5,2.5);
            \coordinate (u1) at (4.7,1.7);
            \coordinate (v1) at (6.3,1.7);
            \draw (p1) -- (u1) -- (v1) -- (p1);
            \node[vertex, label=above:$p$] at (p1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=left:$u$] at (u1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=right:$v$] at (v1) {};

            % Bottom triangle
            \coordinate (x1) at (4.7,0.3);
            \coordinate (y1) at (6.3,0.3);
            \coordinate (q1) at (5.5,-0.8);
            \draw (x1) -- (y1) -- (q1) -- (x1);
            \node[vertex, label=left:$x$] at (x1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=right:$y$] at (y1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:$q$] at (q1) {};

            % First closed trail C_1
            \coordinate (u2) at (4.6,-2.4);
            \coordinate (x2) at (5.1,-3.5);
            \coordinate (y2) at (6.3,-3.0);
            \coordinate (v2) at (6.0,-1.8);
            \draw (u2) -- (x2) -- (v2) -- (y2) -- (u2);
            \node[vertex, label=left:$u$] at (u2) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:$x$] at (x2) {};
            \node[vertex, label=right:$y$] at (y2) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:$v$] at (v2) {};
            \node at (5.5,-4.2) {$C_1=(u,x,v,y,u)$ redrawn};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    Choose the first closed trail to be
    \[
        C_1 = (u,x,v,y,u),
    \]
    which uses the four edges $ux$, $xv$, $vy$, and $yu$. This is the closed trail: start at $u$, keep following unused edges, and because every time we enter a vertex we can also leave it again, the trail cannot get stuck anywhere except at the starting vertex. Hence the walk comes back to $u$ and closes up.

    Now remove the edge set $E_1 = \{ux, xv, vy, yu\}$. The remaining graph $G' = (V, E \setminus E_1)$ has exactly two non-trivial components: the top triangle $(u,p,v,u)$ and the bottom triangle $(x,q,y,x)$. Every vertex in these components still has even degree, so by the induction hypothesis each component has its own Eulerian cycle.

    Finally, splice those smaller cycles into $C_1$. Start to traverse $C_1$ at $u$, but before leaving $u$ for the edge $ux$, first run through the top triangle $(u,p,v,u)$. When the tour later reaches $x$, run through the bottom triangle $(x,q,y,x)$, and then continue along the original trail. One Eulerian cycle of the whole graph is therefore
    \[
        (u,p,v,u,x,q,y,x,v,y,u).
    \]
    This illustrates the sentence ``traverse $C_1$ until $v$, then traverse all of $C_2$, then continue $C_1$ from $v$'': each smaller Eulerian cycle is inserted at a shared vertex, and after all insertions every edge of the original graph has been used exactly once.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
    A Hamiltonion cycle is a closed walk which uses every vertex precisely once. A graph is called Hamiltonian if it contains a Hamiltonian cycle.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    Let $G = (V, E)$ be a simple graph on $n \ge 3$ vertices. If for every vertex $v \in V$, $\deg(v) \ge \frac{n+1}{2}$, then $G$ is Hamiltonian.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    First, we show that $G$ is connected. Suppose for a contradiction that $G$ is disconnected. Let $C$ be the smallest connected component of $G$. The number of vertices in $C$ is at most $\lfloor \frac{n}{2} \rfloor$. Thus, the maximum degree of any vertex in $C$ is at most $\lfloor \frac{n}{2} \rfloor - 1 < \frac{n+1}{2}$, which contradicts the degree condition. Hence, $G$ is connected.

    We proceed by contradiction. Assume that $G$ is not Hamiltonian. Let $P = v_1 v_2 \dots v_k$ be a longest path in $G$. Since $G$ is connected and $n \ge 3$, we have $k \ge 3$.

    Because $P$ is a longest path, all neighbors of its endpoints $v_1$ and $v_k$ must lie within the set $\{v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k\}$. If they had neighbors outside $P$, we could extend $P$ to a longer path, contradicting its maximality.

    Consider the sets of indices of the neighbors of $v_1$ and $v_k$ on the path $P$:
    \begin{align*}
        S & = \{ i \in \{1, 2, \dots, k-1\} \mid \{v_1, v_{i+1}\} \in E \} \\
        T & = \{ i \in \{1, 2, \dots, k-1\} \mid \{v_i, v_k\} \in E \}
    \end{align*}
    Note that $|S| = \deg(v_1)$ and $|T| = \deg(v_k)$. By the given condition, we have:
    $$|S| + |T| = \deg(v_1) + \deg(v_k) \ge \frac{n+1}{2} + \frac{n+1}{2} = n + 1$$
    Since both $S$ and $T$ are subsets of $\{1, 2, \dots, k-1\}$, and $k \le n$, the maximum possible size of their union is $k - 1 \le n - 1$.

    By the Principle of Inclusion-Exclusion (or the Pigeonhole Principle), we have:
    $$|S \cap T| = |S| + |T| - |S \cup T| \ge (n + 1) - (n - 1) = 2 > 0$$
    Therefore, the intersection $S \cap T$ is non-empty. There exists some index $i$ ($1 \le i < k$) such that $\{v_1, v_{i+1}\} \in E$ and $\{v_i, v_k\} \in E$.

    Using these two edges, we can construct a cycle $C$ containing exactly the vertices of $P$:
    $$C = v_1 v_2 \dots v_i v_k v_{k-1} \dots v_{i+1} v_1$$
    Since we assumed $G$ is not Hamiltonian, $C$ does not contain all vertices of $G$, so $k < n$. Because $G$ is connected, there must exist a vertex $u \notin V(C)$ that is adjacent to some vertex $v_j \in V(C)$.

    Since $v_j$ is on the cycle $C$, we can break the cycle by removing one of the edges incident to $v_j$ in $C$, say $\{v_{j-1}, v_j\}$. We then add the edge $\{u, v_j\}$ to form a new path:
    $$P' = u v_j v_{j+1} \dots v_k \dots v_1 \dots v_{j-1}$$
    The length of $P'$ is $k+1$, which strictly exceeds the length of $P$. This contradicts the assumption that $P$ is a longest path in $G$.

    Thus, our assumption that $G$ is not Hamiltonian must be false. $G$ contains a Hamiltonian cycle.
\end{proof}



\begin{remark}
    The statement ``Random graphs are Hamiltonian'' asserts that a random graph $G(n, p)$ contains a Hamiltonian cycle asymptotically almost surely (a.a.s.) provided the edge probability $p$ exceeds a sharp threshold. Specifically, if
    \[
        p \ge \frac{\ln n + \ln \ln n + \omega(1)}{n},
    \]
    then
    \[
        \lim_{n \to \infty} \mathbb{P}(G(n, p) \text{ is Hamiltonian}) = 1.
    \]
    This contrasts with the NP-completeness of the Hamiltonian cycle problem for general graphs.
\end{remark}

The Hamiltonian cycle problem is a fundamental example of an \textbf{NP-complete} problem. In the context of computational complexity:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{P (Polynomial time)} represents the class of problems that can be solved efficiently (in polynomial time) by a deterministic computer (Turing machine).
    \item \textbf{NP (Nondeterministic Polynomial time)} consists of problems where a solution, once guessed, can be verified efficiently. Equivalently, these are solvable by a non-deterministic Turing machine in polynomial time.
    \item \textbf{NP-complete} problems are the ``hardest'' problems in NP. A problem is NP-complete if it is in NP and every other problem in NP can be transformed (reduced) into it in polynomial time.
\end{itemize}
If an efficient algorithm exists for any NP-complete problem, then efficient algorithms exist for all problems in NP (i.e., $\text{P} = \text{NP}$).

\textbf{Graph theory} is a fundamental branch of mathematics that studies the properties of graphs, which are mathematical structures used to model pairwise relations between objects. A graph consists of vertices (also called nodes or points) connected by edges (also called links or lines). The field has grown tremendously since Euler's solution to the Königsberg bridge problem in 1736, and now encompasses numerous subdisciplines:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Structural graph theory} investigates the intrinsic properties and structural characterizations of graphs. Central topics include graph decompositions, minors, and the celebrated Graph Minor Theorem of Robertson and Seymour. This area explores fundamental questions about graph embeddings, planarity, and forbidden subgraph characterizations.

    \item \textbf{Algorithmic graph theory} focuses on the design and analysis of algorithms for graph problems. It addresses computational complexity, approximation algorithms, and efficient data structures for graph representation. Classic problems include shortest paths, maximum flow, graph coloring, and graph isomorphism. The interplay between structural properties and algorithmic efficiency is a key theme.

    \item \textbf{Extremal graph theory} studies the extremal (maximum or minimum) values of graph invariants under given constraints. A prototypical question is: what is the maximum number of edges in a graph on $n$ vertices that does not contain a specified subgraph? The foundational Turán's theorem and the Erdős--Stone theorem are cornerstones of this field.

    \item \textbf{Spectral graph theory} explores connections between graph properties and the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of associated matrices (adjacency matrix, Laplacian matrix). It provides powerful algebraic tools for studying graph connectivity, expansion properties, and random walks on graphs.

    \item \textbf{Random graph theory} investigates properties of graphs generated by random processes, such as the Erdős--Rényi model $G(n,p)$. It studies phase transitions, threshold phenomena, and the typical behavior of graph parameters.

    \item \textbf{Algebraic graph theory} applies algebraic methods---including group theory, linear algebra, and representation theory---to study graphs. Topics include graph automorphisms, Cayley graphs, and association schemes.
\end{itemize}

These subfields are deeply interconnected and find applications across computer science, physics, biology, social network analysis, and numerous other disciplines.

\section{Tree}

\begin{definition}
    A connected graph with no cycles is called a tree.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    This is an example of a tree. It is "木" in Chinese.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
            & \bullet \arrow[d, no head]                                                             &         \\
            \bullet \arrow[r, no head] & \bullet \arrow[r, no head] \arrow[ld, no head] \arrow[rd, no head] \arrow[dd, no head] & \bullet \\
            \bullet                    &                                                                                        & \bullet \\
            & \bullet                                                                                &
        \end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
    Disjoint union of trees is called a forest.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    This is an example of a forest. It is "林" in Chinese.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
            & \bullet \arrow[d, no head]                                                             &         &                            & \bullet \arrow[d, no head]                                          &                            \\
            \bullet \arrow[r, no head] & \bullet \arrow[r, no head] \arrow[ld, no head] \arrow[rd, no head] \arrow[dd, no head] & \bullet & \bullet \arrow[r, no head] & \bullet \arrow[ld, no head] \arrow[rd, no head] \arrow[dd, no head] & \bullet \arrow[l, no head] \\
            \bullet                    &                                                                                        & \bullet & \bullet                    &                                                                     & \bullet                    \\
            & \bullet                                                                                &         &                            & \bullet                                                             &
        \end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Spanning tree]
    Let $G=(V,E)$ be a connected (finite) graph. A \emph{spanning tree} of $G$ is a subgraph
    $T=(V,E_T)$ of $G$ such that
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $V(T)=V(G)$ (so $T$ is spanning), and
        \item $T$ is a tree (equivalently: $T$ is connected and contains no cycles).
    \end{enumerate}
    Equivalently, a spanning tree is a spanning, connected, acyclic subgraph of $G$ (hence $|E_T|=|V|-1$).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    Every connected (finite) graph $G=(V,E)$ has a spanning tree.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let $G=(V,E)$ be connected. Among all spanning subgraphs $H=(V,E_H)$ of $G$ that are connected,
    choose one with $|E_H|$ minimal (such an $H$ exists since $E$ is finite and $G$ itself is admissible).
    We claim that $H$ is acyclic.

    Indeed, if $H$ contains a cycle $C$, pick an edge $e\in E(C)$ and let $H':=(V,E_H\setminus\{e\})$.
    Then $H'$ is still connected, because the endpoints of $e$ remain joined by the rest of the cycle,
    and any path using $e$ can be rerouted along $C\setminus\{e\}$. This contradicts the minimality of $|E_H|$.

    Hence $H$ is connected and acyclic, so $H$ is a tree. Since $V(H)=V(G)$, $H$ is a spanning tree of $G$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    The followings are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $ G=(V,E) $ is a tree.
        \item Any two vertices are connected by a unique path.
        \item $ G $ is connected with $ |V|-1=|E| $.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \begin{description}
        \item[$(1) \implies (2)$] If two vertices $x, y$ are connected by two distinct paths, then their union contains a cycle.
        \item[$(2) \implies (1)$] If $G$ contains a cycle, then any two distinct vertices on the cycle are connected by at least two different paths. Connectivity is given.
        \item[$(1) \implies (3)$] We proceed by induction on $|V|$. The base case $|V|=1$ is trivial. For $|V|>1$, let $P=(u, u_1, \dots, u_l)$ be a longest path. Since $G$ is a tree, all neighbors of the endpoint $u$ must lie on $P$, which implies $u$ is a leaf (degree 1). Removing this leaf and its incident edge leaves a tree on $|V|-1$ vertices, and the result follows by induction.
        \item[$(3) \implies (1)$] Since $G$ is connected, it has a spanning tree $T$. We know $|E(T)| = |V(T)| - 1 = |V(G)| - 1$. The given condition is $|E(G)| = |V(G)| - 1$. Since $T$ is a subgraph of $G$, we must have $E(G) = E(T)$, which implies $G=T$. Thus, $G$ is a tree.
    \end{description}
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    Let $ G $ be a forest, then $ \sharp $ connected components of $ G =|V(G)|-|E(G)|$ .
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Let $G$ be a forest, and let its connected components be
    \[
        G_1,\dots,G_c,
    \]
    where $c$ is the number of connected components.  Set
    \[
        n_i:=|V(G_i)|,\qquad m_i:=|E(G_i)|.
    \]
    Since $G$ is a forest, each $G_i$ is a tree. A basic fact about trees is that a tree on $n_i$ vertices has exactly $n_i-1$ edges, i.e.
    \[
        m_i=n_i-1\qquad (1\le i\le c).
    \]
    Summing over all components gives
    \[
        |E(G)|=\sum_{i=1}^c m_i=\sum_{i=1}^c (n_i-1)=\sum_{i=1}^c n_i - c = |V(G)|-c.
    \]
    Rearranging yields
    \[
        c=|V(G)|-|E(G)|,
    \]
    as claimed.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    Let $ T $ be a tree, $ n:= |V(T)| \ge 2 $ with degree sequence $(d_1, \ldots, d_n)$, ordered by non-increasing. For example:

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}
            \node[vertex] (c) at (0,0) {};
            \node[vertex] (t) at (0,1) {};
            \node[vertex] (r) at (1,0) {};
            \node[vertex] (m) at (-1,-1) {};
            \node[vertex] (b) at (-2,-2) {};
            \draw (c) -- (t) (c) -- (r) (c) -- (m) (m) -- (b);
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    The degree sequence is $ (3,2,1,1,1) $.

\end{example}

What is the number of trees on n vertices? $ t(n) $. Cayley's formula solved this problem.

\begin{example}
    There are the basic examples:
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[vertex/.style={circle, fill=black, inner sep=1.5pt, outer sep=0pt}, every label/.style={font=\small}]
            % n=2
            \node at (-1.2, 0.3) {$n=2$};
            \begin{scope}[xshift=0cm]
                \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 1}] (a) at (0,0) {};
                \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 2}] (b) at (1,0) {};
                \draw (a) -- (b);
            \end{scope}
            \node at (2, 0.3) {$t(2)=1$};

            % n=3
            \node at (-1.2, -1.7) {$n=3$};
            \begin{scope}[xshift=0cm, yshift=-1.5cm]
                \node[vertex, label=above left:{\scriptsize 1}] (a1) at (0,0) {};
                \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 2}] (b1) at (1,0) {};
                \node[vertex, label=right:{\scriptsize 3}] (c1) at (1,-1) {};
                \draw (a1) -- (b1) -- (c1);
            \end{scope}
            \begin{scope}[xshift=2.2cm, yshift=-1.5cm]
                \node[vertex, label=above left:{\scriptsize 1}] (a2) at (0,0) {};
                \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 3}] (b2) at (1,0) {};
                \node[vertex, label=right:{\scriptsize 2}] (c2) at (1,-1) {};
                \draw (a2) -- (b2) -- (c2);
            \end{scope}
            \begin{scope}[xshift=4.4cm, yshift=-1.5cm]
                \node[vertex, label=above left:{\scriptsize 2}] (a3) at (0,0) {};
                \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 1}] (b3) at (1,0) {};
                \node[vertex, label=right:{\scriptsize 3}] (c3) at (1,-1) {};
                \draw (a3) -- (b3) -- (c3);
            \end{scope}
            \node at (6.8, -2.4) {$t(3)=3$};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
    $t(n)=n^{n-2} $.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \textbf{Proof 1:}
    Goal: find a bijection between trees on sets $ [n] $ and $ [n]^{n-2} $.

    Let $ T $ be a tree

    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Pick $ u_1 \in u= \mathrm{min}\{ v \in V: \deg (v)=1  \} $ and $ a_1 :=  $ the unique neighbor of $ u_1 $.
        \item Remove $ u_1 $, write down the lable of $ a_1 $, and repeat.
    \end{enumerate}

    For example:
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[vertex/.style={circle, fill=black, inner sep=1.5pt, outer sep=0pt}]
            \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 2}] (2) at (0,1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:{\scriptsize 1}] (1) at (0,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 3}] (3) at (-1,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:{\scriptsize 5}] (5) at (1,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:{\scriptsize 4}] (4) at (-1,-1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 6}] (6) at (1,1) {};
            \draw (3) -- (1) -- (5) (1) -- (2) (3) -- (4) (5) -- (6);
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    We pick $u_1=2$, $a_1=1$, and remove $u_1$. Then we pick $u_2=4$, $a_2=3$, and remove $u_2$. Then we pick $u_3=3$, $a_3=1$, and remove $u_3$. Then we pick $u_4=1$, $a_4=5$, and remove $u_4$. Now we get a sequence $ a=(1,3,1,5) $ in $ [6]^{4} $.


    After all, we get a sequence $ a=(a_1, \ldots, a_{n-2}) $ in $ [n]^{n-2} $. Next we want to show: For eah sequence $ a $ in $ [n]^{n-2} $, there exists a unique tree $ T $.

    Let $ \mathrm{d_i} := \sharp $ degree of vertex $ i $ and $ f_i := \sharp $ occurrences of $ i $ in $ a $.

    Note that $ f_i \le d_i -1 $ for all $ i $. Hence $ n-2=\sum f_i \le \sum (d_i-1)=(2n-2)-n =n-2 $. Hence $ f_i=d_i -1 $ for all $ i $.

    Now we construct $ T $:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item For $ i=1,\ldots,n-2 $: choose the smallest $ b_i \in [n] $ not in $ (a_i, a_{i+1}, \ldots, a_{n-2}) $ and not equal to any $ b_j $ ($ j<i $); add edge $ (a_i, b_i) $.
        \item So far we have $ n-2 $ edges. The two vertices that never appear as $ b_1,\ldots,b_{n-2} $ are exactly the last ``remaining'' pair; add the edge between them to get the $ (n-1) $-th edge. Thus we obtain a tree $ T $.
    \end{enumerate}

    For example: given $ a=(1,3,1,5) $, we can construct the tree step by step :
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[vertex/.style={circle, fill=black, inner sep=1.5pt, outer sep=0pt}]
            \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 2}] (2) at (0,1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:{\scriptsize 1}] (1) at (0,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 3}] (3) at (-1,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:{\scriptsize 5}] (5) at (1,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:{\scriptsize 4}] (4) at (-1,-1) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:{\scriptsize 6}] (6) at (1,1) {};
            \draw (3) -- (1) -- (5) (1) -- (2) (3) -- (4) (5) -- (6);
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    Hence, we have a bijection between trees on sets $ [n] $ and $ [n]^{n-2} $.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}
    \textbf{Proof 2:}
    Let $d_1,\dots,d_n$ be positive integers with $\sum_{i=1}^n d_i=2(n-1)$, and let
    \[
        t(d_1,\dots,d_n):=\#\{\text{labeled trees on }[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}\text{ with }\deg(i)=d_i\}.
    \]
    We first prove the degree-sequence formula
    \[
        t(d_1,\dots,d_n)=\binom{n-2}{d_1-1,\dots,d_n-1}
        =\frac{(n-2)!}{\prod_{i=1}^n (d_i-1)!}.
        \tag{$\ast$}
    \]

    \noindent\emph{Step 1: a leaf-removal recurrence.}
    Assume $d_n=1$ (we may relabel so that one leaf is vertex $n$). In any such tree, the leaf $n$
    is adjacent to a unique vertex $i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}$. Removing $n$ and its incident edge yields a
    labeled tree on $[n-1]$ whose degree sequence is
    \[
        (d_1,\dots,d_i-1,\dots,d_{n-1}).
    \]
    Conversely, given a labeled tree on $[n-1]$ with degrees $(d_1,\dots,d_i-1,\dots,d_{n-1})$,
    attaching the new leaf $n$ to $i$ produces a labeled tree on $[n]$ with degrees
    $(d_1,\dots,d_{n-1},1)$. Hence
    \[
        t(d_1,\dots,d_{n-1},1)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} t(d_1,\dots,d_i-1,\dots,d_{n-1}),
        \tag{R}
    \]
    where terms with $d_i=1$ are understood to contribute $0$.

    \noindent\emph{Step 2: verification for the multinomial expression.}
    Define
    \[
        F(d_1,\dots,d_n):=\frac{(n-2)!}{\prod_{j=1}^n (d_j-1)!}.
    \]
    For $d_n=1$ we have
    \[
        F(d_1,\dots,d_{n-1},1)=\frac{(n-2)!}{\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}(d_j-1)!}.
    \]
    Moreover, for $1\le i\le n-1$,
    \[
        F(d_1,\dots,d_i-1,\dots,d_{n-1})
        =\frac{(n-3)!}{(d_i-2)!\prod_{j\ne i,\, j\le n-1}(d_j-1)!}
        =\frac{(n-3)!(d_i-1)}{\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}(d_j-1)!}.
    \]
    Summing over $i$ gives
    \[
        \sum_{i=1}^{n-1}F(d_1,\dots,d_i-1,\dots,d_{n-1})
        =\frac{(n-3)!}{\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}(d_j-1)!}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_i-1).
    \]
    Since $\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} d_i = 2(n-1)-1=2n-3$, we have
    \[
        \sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_i-1)=(2n-3)-(n-1)=n-2,
    \]
    and therefore the right-hand side equals
    \[
        \frac{(n-3)!(n-2)}{\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}(d_j-1)!}
        =\frac{(n-2)!}{\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}(d_j-1)!}
        =F(d_1,\dots,d_{n-1},1).
    \]
    Thus $F$ satisfies the same recurrence as $t$.

    \noindent\emph{Step 3: induction.}
    For $n=2$ there is exactly one labeled tree, so $t(1,1)=1=F(1,1)$. By induction on $n$ using
    the recurrence, we conclude $t(d_1,\dots,d_n)=F(d_1,\dots,d_n)$.

    \noindent\emph{Step 4: summing over degree sequences.}
    Let $t(n)$ be the total number of labeled trees on $[n]$. Summing over all degree
    sequences with $\sum d_i=2(n-1)$ and setting $r_i=d_i-1$ yields
    \[
        t(n)=\sum_{\substack{r_1,\dots,r_n\ge 0\\ r_1+\cdots+r_n=n-2}}
        \binom{n-2}{r_1,\dots,r_n}.
    \]
    By the multinomial theorem,
    \[
        (x_1+\cdots+x_n)^{\,n-2}
        =\sum_{\substack{r_1+\cdots+r_n=n-2}}
        \binom{n-2}{r_1,\dots,r_n}x_1^{r_1}\cdots x_n^{r_n}.
    \]
    Evaluating at $x_1=\cdots=x_n=1$ gives
    \[
        n^{n-2}
        =\sum_{\substack{r_1+\cdots+r_n=n-2}}
        \binom{n-2}{r_1,\dots,r_n}
        =t(n).
    \]
    Hence $t(n)=n^{n-2}$, which is Cayley's formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}
    \textbf{Proof 3:} Using Kirchhoff's Matrix--Tree Theorem.

    Let $t(n)$ denote the number of spanning trees of the complete graph $K_n$.
    Write $A$ for the adjacency matrix and $D$ for the diagonal degree matrix. The (combinatorial)
    Laplacian is
    \[
        L(K_n)=D-A.
    \]
    In $K_n$ every vertex has degree $n-1$, hence $D=(n-1)I$. Moreover $A=J-I$, where $J$ is the
    all-ones matrix. Therefore
    \[
        L(K_n)=(n-1)I-(J-I)=nI-J.
    \]

    We determine the eigenvalues of $L(K_n)$. Let $\mathbf{1}=(1,\dots,1)^{\mathsf T}$. Since
    $J\mathbf{1}=n\mathbf{1}$, we have
    \[
        L(K_n)\mathbf{1}=(nI-J)\mathbf{1}=n\mathbf{1}-n\mathbf{1}=0,
    \]
    so $0$ is an eigenvalue (with eigenvector $\mathbf{1}$).
    If $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$ satisfies $\mathbf{1}^{\mathsf T}x=\sum_{i=1}^n x_i=0$, then each entry of
    $Jx$ equals $\sum_{i=1}^n x_i=0$, hence $Jx=0$, and thus
    \[
        L(K_n)x=(nI-J)x=nx.
    \]
    Consequently, $n$ is an eigenvalue on the $(n-1)$-dimensional subspace
    $\mathbf{1}^\perp=\{x:\sum_i x_i=0\}$, so the spectrum of $L(K_n)$ is
    \[
        \underbrace{n,\dots,n}_{n-1\text{ times}},\quad 0.
    \]

    By Kirchhoff's Matrix--Tree Theorem (eigenvalue form), if the Laplacian eigenvalues are
    $0=\lambda_n<\lambda_1\le\cdots\le\lambda_{n-1}$, then the number of spanning trees is
    \[
        \tau(G)=\frac{1}{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\lambda_i.
    \]
    Applying this to $G=K_n$ gives
    \[
        t(n)=\tau(K_n)=\frac{1}{n}\cdot n^{\,n-1}=n^{\,n-2}.
    \]
    This is Cayley's formula.
\end{proof}
\chapter{Lecture 3}

\section{Chromatic number}

\begin{definition}
    Let $G=(V,E)$ be a graph and let $C$ be a set of colors.
    A \emph{proper coloring} of $G$ is a map
    \[
        f: V \to C
    \]
    such that for every edge $ \{x,y\} \in E$ (equivalently, for every pair of adjacent
    vertices $x \sim y$) we have
    \[
        f(x) \neq f(y).
    \]

    If $G$ admits a proper coloring using at most $k$ colors (that is, $|C|=k$),
    then $G$ is called \emph{$k$-colorable}.

    The \emph{chromatic number} of $G$, denoted by $\chi(G)$, is the minimum number
    of colors needed for a proper coloring of $G$:
    \[
        \chi(G) = \min \{\, k : G \text{ is } k\text{-colorable} \}.
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    Consider following graph:
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=2pt, minimum size=6pt},
                vertex mag/.style={vertex, fill=magenta},
                vertex red/.style={vertex, fill=red},
                vertex blue/.style={vertex, fill=blue},
                vertex green/.style={vertex, fill=green}]
            % 屋顶顶点 (顶端)
            \node[vertex mag] (top) at (0.5, 1.5) {};
            % 正方形上边两顶点
            \node[vertex red]  (tl) at (0, 1) {};
            \node[vertex blue] (tr) at (1, 1) {};
            % 正方形下边两顶点
            \node[vertex mag] (bl) at (0, 0) {};
            \node[vertex green] (br) at (1, 0) {};
            % 屋顶三角形（顶端洋红 top 与右上蓝 tr 的边显式画出）
            \draw (top) -- (tl) -- (tr) -- cycle;
            \draw (top) -- (tr);
            % 正方形周长
            \draw (tl) -- (bl) -- (br) -- (tr);
            % 正方形两条对角线
            \draw (tl) -- (br) (tr) -- (bl);
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}
    We have $\chi(G)=4$.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}
    A graph $G$ is bipartite if and only if $\chi(G)=2$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    ($\Rightarrow$) Suppose $G$ is bipartite. Then $V(G)$ can be written as a disjoint union
    \[
        V(G)=A\cup B,
    \]
    where every edge of $G$ has one endpoint in $A$ and the other in $B$.
    Define a coloring $f:V(G)\to\{1,2\}$ by
    \[
        f(v)=
        \begin{cases}
            1, & v\in A, \\
            2, & v\in B.
        \end{cases}
    \]
    If $\{u,v\} \in E(G)$, then $u$ and $v$ lie in different parts, hence $f(u)\neq f(v)$.
    Thus this is a proper coloring using two colors, so $\chi(G)\le 2$.
    Since $G$ contains an edge, at least two colors are required, hence $\chi(G)=2$.

    ($\Leftarrow$) Suppose $\chi(G)=2$. Then there exists a proper coloring
    \[
        f:V(G)\to\{1,2\}.
    \]
    Let
    \[
        A=f^{-1}(1), \qquad B=f^{-1}(2).
    \]
    Then $V(G)=A\cup B$ and $A\cap B=\varnothing$.
    Since the coloring is proper, every edge $\{u,v\} \in E(G)$ satisfies $f(u)\neq f(v)$,
    so one endpoint lies in $A$ and the other in $B$.
    Hence there are no edges inside $A$ or inside $B$, and $G$ is bipartite.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    Complete bipartite graph $K_{2,3}$:
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=2pt, minimum size=6pt},
                vertex red/.style={vertex, fill=red},
                vertex blue/.style={vertex, fill=blue}]
            % 左侧 2 个红点
            \node[vertex red] (r1) at (0, 0.5) {};
            \node[vertex red] (r2) at (0, -0.5) {};
            % 右侧 3 个蓝点
            \node[vertex blue] (b1) at (1.2, 1) {};
            \node[vertex blue] (b2) at (1.2, 0) {};
            \node[vertex blue] (b3) at (1.2, -1) {};
            % 左部与右部之间全部连边
            \draw (r1) -- (b1) (r1) -- (b2) (r1) -- (b3);
            \draw (r2) -- (b1) (r2) -- (b2) (r2) -- (b3);
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
    If $G$ is planar then $ \chi(G) \le 4 $.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}
    $\chi(K_n)=n$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    In the complete graph $K_n$, every pair of distinct vertices is adjacent.
    Hence in any proper coloring, adjacent vertices must receive different
    colors, so all $n$ vertices must have distinct colors. Thus $\chi(K_n)\ge n$.

    On the other hand, assigning a different color to each vertex gives a
    proper coloring with $n$ colors. Hence $\chi(K_n)\le n$.

    Therefore $\chi(K_n)=n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    For a cycle $C_n$,
    \[
        \chi(C_n)=
        \begin{cases}
            2, & \text{if } n \text{ is even}, \\
            3, & \text{if } n \text{ is odd}.
        \end{cases}
    \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Let $V(C_n)=\{v_1,\ldots,v_n\}$ where $v_i\sim v_{i+1}$ for $1\le i<n$ and
    $v_n\sim v_1$.

    If $n$ is even, color the vertices alternately by two colors along the cycle:
    \[
        f(v_i)=
        \begin{cases}
            1, & i \text{ odd},  \\
            2, & i \text{ even}.
        \end{cases}
    \]
    Since $n$ is even, $v_n$ and $v_1$ receive different colors, so this is a proper
    coloring. Hence $\chi(C_n)\le2$. Since $C_n$ has an edge, $\chi(C_n)\ge2$,
    thus $\chi(C_n)=2$.

    If $n$ is odd, alternating two colors along the cycle forces $v_n$ and $v_1$
    to have the same color, contradicting that they are adjacent. Hence
    $\chi(C_n)\ge3$. Coloring the vertices alternately with two colors and giving
    $v_n$ a third color yields a proper coloring with three colors, so
    $\chi(C_n)\le3$. Therefore $\chi(C_n)=3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    consider the following graphs:
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=2pt, minimum size=6pt},
                vertex blue/.style={vertex, fill=blue},
                vertex mag/.style={vertex, fill=magenta},
                vertex green/.style={vertex, fill=green}]
            % 左图：三角形（上蓝，左下洋红，右下绿）
            \node[vertex blue] (t1) at (0, 1) {};
            \node[vertex mag]  (t2) at (-0.6, 0) {};
            \node[vertex green] (t3) at (0.6, 0) {};
            \draw (t1) -- (t2) -- (t3) -- (t1) -- cycle;
            % 右图：正方形（左上洋红，右上绿，左下绿，右下洋红），仅四边
            \node[vertex mag]  (s1) at (2.5, 1) {};
            \node[vertex green] (s2) at (3.5, 1) {};
            \node[vertex green] (s3) at (2.5, 0) {};
            \node[vertex mag]  (s4) at (3.5, 0) {};
            \draw (s1) -- (s2) -- (s4) -- (s3) -- (s1) -- cycle;
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Brooks' Theorem]
    Let $d \ge 3$ and let $G$ be a graph with maximum degree $d$.
    If $K_{d+1}$ is not a subgraph of $G$, then
    \[
        \chi(G) \le d .
    \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose, for a contradiction, that the theorem is false.
    Let $G$ be a counterexample with the minimum number of vertices.
    Then $\chi(G) = d+1$.

    Let $x \in V(G)$ and let $N(x) = \{x_1, \dots, x_\ell\}$, where $\ell \le d$.
    Let $H = G - x$. By the minimality of $G$, we have $\chi(H) \le d$.
    Take a proper $d$-coloring of $H$.

    If fewer than $d$ colors appear in $N(x)$, then we can color $x$
    with a color not used by its neighbors, obtaining a $d$-coloring of $G$,
    a contradiction. Hence $\ell = d$ and the vertices $x_1, \dots, x_d$
    receive pairwise distinct colors $1, \dots, d$.

    For $1 \le i < j \le d$, let $H_{ij}$ be the subgraph of $H$
    induced by the vertices colored $i$ or $j$.
    If $x_i$ and $x_j$ lie in different components of $H_{ij}$,
    then exchanging the colors $i$ and $j$ in the component containing $x_i$
    changes the color of $x_i$ to $j$ but leaves $x_j$ unchanged.
    Thus color $i$ disappears from $N(x)$, and we may color $x$ with $i$,
    again obtaining a $d$-coloring of $G$, a contradiction.

    Therefore, $x_i$ and $x_j$ lie in the same component of $H_{ij}$
    for all $i \ne j$. In particular, there exists an $i$--$j$ path between them
    using only colors $i$ and $j$.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=1.2pt}, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            \node[vertex] (x) at (0,0) {$x$};
            \node[vertex] (x1) at (-1.2, 0.8) {$x_1$};
            \node[vertex] (x2) at (1.2, 0.8) {$x_2$};
            \node[vertex] (x3) at (0, -1) {$x_3$};
            \draw (x) -- (x1) (x) -- (x2) (x) -- (x3);
            \draw[thick, gray] (x1) to[bend right=25] (x2);
            \node[above, gray, font=\footnotesize] at (0, 1.35) {$H_{12}$ path};
            \node[above left] at (-1.35, 0.9) {\scriptsize 1};
            \node[above right] at (1.35, 0.9) {\scriptsize 2};
            \node[below] at (0, -1.25) {\scriptsize 3};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    If some vertex $y$ in such a component had three neighbors in it,
    it would have at most $d-3$ neighbors of other colors in $H$.
    Consequently, $y$ is adjacent to at most $d-2$ distinct colors in total.
    We can recolor $y$ with a missing color $k \notin \{i, j\}$, which removes $y$ from $H_{ij}$.
    Since $x_i$ must have exactly one neighbor of color $j$ in $H_{ij}$ (otherwise its total degree in $G$ would exceed $d$),
    the component of $H_{ij}$ containing $x_i$ and $x_j$ must be a simple path (a Kempe chain);
    otherwise, recoloring a vertex of degree $\ge 3$ would disconnect $x_i$ and $x_j$, yielding the previous contradiction.

    Since $K_{d+1}$ is not a subgraph of $G$, the vertices in $N(x)$ cannot form a clique.
    Assume without loss of generality that $x_1$ and $x_2$ are not adjacent.
    The path $H_{12}$ connecting $x_1$ and $x_2$ must contain at least one intermediate vertex.
    Let $y$ be the neighbor of $x_1$ on $H_{12}$. The color of $y$ is 2.
    Let $z$ be the other neighbor of $y$ on $H_{12}$, which has color 1.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95, vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=1.2pt}, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            \node[vertex] (x1) at (0,0) {$x_1$};
            \node[vertex] (y) at (1.2,0) {$y$};
            \node[vertex] (z) at (2.4,0) {$z$};
            \node at (3.2,0) {$\cdots$};
            \node[vertex] (x2) at (4,0) {$x_2$};
            \draw (x1) -- (y) -- (z) -- (3.2,0) -- (x2);
            \node[above] at (0, 0.35) {\scriptsize color 1};
            \node[above] at (1.2, 0.35) {\scriptsize 2};
            \node[above] at (2.4, 0.35) {\scriptsize 1};
            \node[above] at (4, 0.35) {\scriptsize 2};
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (2, -0.5) {path $H_{12}$};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    Now, consider the Kempe chain $H_{13}$ connecting $x_1$ and $x_3$.
    We interchange the colors 1 and 3 on $H_{13}$.
    After this swap, $x_1$ receives color 3. The vertex $y$ retains color 2, as it is not in $H_{13}$.
    To avoid a contradiction, $x_1$ and $x_2$ must still be connected by a new simple path $H'_{23}$.
    This path must start with the edge $x_1y$, meaning $y$ must now have a second neighbor of color 3 to continue the path.

    In the original coloring, $y$ has exactly two neighbors of color 1 ($x_1$ and $z$)
    and exactly one neighbor of each remaining color $3, \dots, d$.
    For $y$ to gain a second neighbor of color 3 after the swap on $H_{13}$,
    its neighbor $z$ must have belonged to $H_{13}$ and changed its color from 1 to 3.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=1.2pt}, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            \node[vertex] (y) at (0,0) {$y$};
            \node[vertex] (x1) at (-1, 0.6) {$x_1$};
            \node[vertex] (z) at (-1, -0.6) {$z$};
            \draw (y) -- (x1) (y) -- (z);
            \node[above] at (-1, 0.95) {\scriptsize 1};
            \node[below] at (-1, -0.95) {\scriptsize 1};
            \node[right] at (0.4, 0) {\scriptsize 2};
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (0, -1.1) {before: $y$'s neighbors in $H_{12}$};
        \end{tikzpicture}
        \qquad
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=1.2pt}, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            \node[vertex] (y) at (0,0) {$y$};
            \node[vertex] (x1) at (-1, 0.6) {$x_1$};
            \node[vertex] (z) at (-1, -0.6) {$z$};
            \draw (y) -- (x1) (y) -- (z);
            \node[above] at (-1, 0.95) {\scriptsize 3};
            \node[below] at (-1, -0.95) {\scriptsize 3};
            \node[right] at (0.4, 0) {\scriptsize 2};
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (0, -1.1) {after swap on $H_{13}$};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    However, if $z \in H_{13}$, then $z$ must have at least two neighbors of color 3 (to be in $H_{13}$),
    two neighbors of color 2 (to be in $H_{12}$), and at least one neighbor of each of the remaining $d-3$ colors
    (to prevent it from being easily recolored).
    This requires $\deg(z) \ge 2 + 2 + (d-3) = d+1$, which contradicts $\max_{v \in V(G)} \deg(v) = d$.

    This final contradiction demonstrates that no such minimal counterexample $G$ can exist, completing the proof.
\end{proof}

\section{Ramsey theorem}

A classical motivation for Ramsey theory comes from the following social
question posed in the 1950s: in any group of six people, there must exist
either three people who all know each other or three people who are
pairwise strangers.

This can be modeled using graph theory. Consider the complete graph
$K_6$ whose vertices represent the six people. Color an edge red if the
two corresponding people know each other and blue otherwise. Pick any
vertex $x$. Among the five edges incident with $x$, at least three must
have the same color (since $5=3+2$). Suppose, without loss of generality,
that $x$ has at least three red neighbors $a,b,c$.
\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85, vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=1.2pt}, every node/.style={font=\small}]
        \node[vertex] (x) at (0,0) {$x$};
        \node[vertex] (a) at (1.4, 0.9) {$a$};
        \node[vertex] (b) at (1.5, -0.3) {$b$};
        \node[vertex] (c) at (1.2, -1) {$c$};
        \draw[red, thick] (x) -- (a) (x) -- (b) (x) -- (c);
        \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (0.75, -1.35) {three red neighbors};
    \end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

If any pair among $\{a,b,c\}$ is joined by a red edge, say $ab$, then
$\{x,a,b\}$ forms a red triangle. Otherwise the edges $ab,bc,ca$ are all
blue, and $\{a,b,c\}$ forms a blue triangle. Hence every red--blue
coloring of the edges of $K_6$ contains a monochromatic triangle.
\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=1.2pt}, every node/.style={font=\small}]
        \node[vertex] (x) at (0,0) {$x$};
        \node[vertex] (a) at (1.2, 0.7) {$a$};
        \node[vertex] (b) at (1.2, -0.7) {$b$};
        \draw[red, thick] (x) -- (a) -- (b) -- (x);
        \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (0.6, -1) {red triangle $\{x,a,b\}$};
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \hspace{2cm}
    \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, vertex/.style={circle, draw=black, inner sep=1.2pt}, every node/.style={font=\small}]
        \node[vertex] (a) at (90:0.8) {$a$};
        \node[vertex] (b) at (210:0.8) {$b$};
        \node[vertex] (c) at (330:0.8) {$c$};
        \draw[blue, thick] (a) -- (b) -- (c) -- (a);
        \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (0, -1.15) {blue triangle $\{a,b,c\}$};
    \end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

This result is written as
\[
    R(3,3)=6,
\]
where $R(s,t)$ denotes the smallest integer $n$ such that every
red--blue coloring of the edges of $K_n$ contains either a red $K_s$
or a blue $K_t$. The study of such unavoidable patterns in sufficiently
large structures forms the basis of \emph{Ramsey theory}.

$R(s,t)$ is the smallest integer n such that all edge colorings with 2 colors $\{ c_1,c_2 \}$ of $K_n$ contain a monochromatic $K_s$ in color $c_1$ or a monochromatic $K_t$ in color $c_2$= smallest integer such that all graphs G on $n$ vertices satisfies

(1) $K_s$ is a subgraph of G or

(2) $K_t$ is subgraph of $\bar{G}$

\begin{corollary}
    $R(2,t)=R(t,2)=t$ for all $t \ge 2$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    We prove $R(2,t)=t$; the equality $R(t,2)=t$ follows by symmetry.

    \emph{Upper bound.} In any red--blue coloring of $E(K_t)$, either there is a red edge, which is a red $K_2$, or else there are no red edges, in which case all edges are blue and the whole vertex set forms a blue $K_t$. Hence $R(2,t)\le t$.

    \emph{Lower bound.} Consider $K_{t-1}$ with all edges colored blue. Then there is no red $K_2$, and also no blue $K_t$ (since there are only $t-1$ vertices). Thus $R(2,t)\ge t$.

    Therefore $R(2,t)=t$, and hence $R(t,2)=t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    $R(s,t) \le R(s-1,t)+R(s,t-1)$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let
    \[
        n:=R(s-1,t)+R(s,t-1),
    \]
    and consider any red--blue coloring of the edges of $K_n$.
    Fix a red vertex $x$. Let $A$ be the set of red neighbors of $x$ and $B$ the
    set of blue neighbors of $x$. Then $|A|+|B|=n-1$.

    If $|A|\ge R(s-1,t)$, then the induced subgraph on $A$ contains either a
    red $K_{s-1}$ or a blue $K_t$. In the first case, together with $x$ this
    forms a red $K_s$; in the second case we already have a blue $K_t$.

    If $|A|<R(s-1,t)$, then
    \[
        |B|=n-1-|A|\ge n-1-(R(s-1,t)-1)=R(s,t-1).
    \]
    as $n=R(s-1,t)+R(s,t-1)$
    Hence the induced subgraph on $B$ contains either a red $K_s$ or a blue
    $K_{t-1}$. In the first case we already have a red $K_s$; in the second
    case, together with $x$ this forms a blue $K_t$.

    Thus every coloring of $K_n$ contains a red $K_s$ or a blue $K_t$, so
    $R(s,t)\le n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    $R(r,s) \le \binom{r+s-2}{r-1}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    By induction on $r+s$. The base case $r+s=3$ is trivial. For the inductive step,
    assume the inequality holds for all $r+s'<r+s$. Then
    \[
        R(r,s)\le R(r-1,s)+R(r,s-1)\le \binom{r+s-3}{r-2}+\binom{r+s-3}{s-2}.
    \]
    By the inductive hypothesis,
    \[
        \binom{r+s-3}{r-2}\le \binom{r+s-2}{r-1} \quad \text{and} \quad \binom{r+s-3}{s-2}\le \binom{r+s-2}{s-1}.
    \]
    Hence $R(r,s)\le \binom{r+s-2}{r-1}$.
\end{proof}

We first establish a recursive upper bound for the general Ramsey number $R(r, s)$, from which the diagonal bound follows.

\begin{theorem}[Erd\H{o}s-Szekeres Upper Bound]
    For any integers $s \ge 2$, the diagonal Ramsey number satisfies
    $$ R(s, s) \le \binom{2s - 2}{s - 1}. $$
    Consequently, as $s \to \infty$, we have the asymptotic bound
    $$ R(s, s) \le (1 + o(1)) \frac{4^{s-1}}{\sqrt{\pi s}}. $$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    The first part follows from the proof of the general bound $R(r, s) \le \binom{r+s-2}{r-1}$.

    \textbf{Asymptotic Analysis:} To derive the asymptotic behavior, we apply Stirling's approximation, $n! \sim \sqrt{2\pi n} \left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^n$:
    $$ \binom{2s - 2}{s - 1} = \frac{(2s-2)!}{((s-1)!)^2} \sim \frac{\sqrt{2\pi(2s-2)} \left(\frac{2s-2}{e}\right)^{2s-2}}{2\pi(s-1) \left(\frac{s-1}{e}\right)^{2s-2}} = \frac{4^{s-1}}{\sqrt{\pi(s-1)}}. $$
    As $s \to \infty$, $\frac{1}{\sqrt{s-1}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}(1+o(1))$, yielding the desired asymptotic form:
    $$ R(s, s) \le (1 + o(1)) \frac{4^{s-1}}{\sqrt{\pi s}}. $$
\end{proof}

This following theorem introduces the probabilistic method to combinatorics, demonstrating the existence of specific graphs by showing that the probability of their existence is strictly positive.

\begin{theorem}[Erd\H{o}s Exponential Lower Bound]
    For any integer $n \ge s \ge 2$, if
    $$ \binom{n}{s} 2^{1 - \binom{s}{2}} < 1, $$
    then $R(s, s) > n$. Consequently, we have the asymptotic lower bound:
    $$ R(s, s) \ge (1 + o(1)) \frac{s}{e\sqrt{2}} 2^{s/2}. $$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Consider a complete graph $K_n$. We assign colors (red or blue) to each edge of $K_n$ independently and uniformly at random with probability $\frac{1}{2}$.

    Let $S$ be a fixed subset of $V(K_n)$ of size $s$. Let $A_S$ denote the event that the complete subgraph induced by $S$ (i.e., $K_s$) is monochromatic (either all red or all blue). The probability of $A_S$ occurring is:
    $$ \mathbb{P}(A_S) = 2 \cdot \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\binom{s}{2}} = 2^{1 - \binom{s}{2}}. $$

    Let $X$ be the random variable counting the total number of monochromatic $K_s$ subgraphs in $K_n$. We can express $X$ as the sum of indicator variables: $X = \sum_{|S|=s} \mathbf{1}_{A_S}$.
    By the linearity of expectation, the expected number of monochromatic $s$-cliques is:
    $$ \mathbb{E}[X] = \sum_{|S|=s} \mathbb{P}(A_S) = \binom{n}{s} 2^{1 - \binom{s}{2}}. $$

    If $\mathbb{E}[X] < 1$, then by the properties of random variables, there must exist at least one point in the sample space (i.e., at least one specific 2-coloring of $K_n$) for which $X = 0$. Such a coloring contains strictly zero monochromatic $K_s$ subgraphs. By definition, this implies that the Ramsey number $R(s, s)$ must be strictly greater than $n$.

    \textbf{Asymptotic Analysis:} We seek the largest $n$ such that the strict inequality holds. Using the standard upper bound for binomial coefficients $\binom{n}{s} \le \frac{n^s}{s!}$, it is sufficient to satisfy:
    $$ \frac{n^s}{s!} 2^{1 - s(s-1)/2} < 1 \implies n^s < s! \cdot 2^{s(s-1)/2 - 1}. $$
    Taking the $s$-th root of both sides gives:
    $$ n < (s!)^{1/s} \cdot 2^{(s-1)/2} \cdot 2^{-1/s}. $$
    Applying Stirling's approximation $(s!)^{1/s} \sim \frac{s}{e} (\sqrt{2\pi s})^{1/s} \sim \frac{s}{e}$, and noting that $2^{(s-1)/2} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} 2^{s/2}$, we obtain:
    $$ n \approx \frac{s}{e} \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} 2^{s/2} = \frac{s}{e\sqrt{2}} 2^{s/2}. $$
    Therefore, setting $n$ to be slightly less than this value guarantees the existence of a valid coloring, establishing the asymptotic lower bound:
    $$ R(s, s) \ge (1 + o(1)) \frac{s}{e\sqrt{2}} 2^{s/2}. $$
\end{proof}

    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}

        \toprule
        \textbf{Result Type} & \textbf{Year} & \textbf{Researchers}              & \textbf{Key Breakthrough}                                                               \\
        \midrule
        Classic Upper Bound  & 1935          & Erd\H{o}s, Szekeres               & $R(s,s) \le D \cdot 4^{s-1}$ (based on combinatorics).                                  \\
        Upper Bound Impr.    & 2009          & David Conlon                      & Sub-exponential improvement: $R(s,s) \le s^{-c \frac{\log s}{\log \log s}} 4^s$.        \\
        Upper Bound Brkthr.  & 2023/24       & Campos, Griffiths, Morris, et al. & First exponential improvement: $R(s,s) \le (4-\epsilon)^s$ ($\epsilon \approx 2^{-7}$). \\
        Classic Lower Bound  & 1947          & Paul Erd\H{o}s                    & $R(s,s) >C \cdot 2^{s/2}$ (dawn of the probabilistic method).                           \\
        Lower Bound Brkthr.  & 2025          & Jie Ma, W. Shen, S. Xie           & Improvement for the off-diagonal Ramsey numbers $R(s, Cs)$.                             \\
        \bottomrule
