\end{table}

For off-diagonal Ramsey numbers, we introduce the following results:

\begin{table}[ht]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
    \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright}p{3.2cm} r >{\raggedright}p{4.5cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
        \toprule
        \textbf{Result Type} & \textbf{Year} & \textbf{Researchers}                               & \textbf{Key Contribution}                                                                                                                              \\
        \midrule
        Classic Upper Bound  & 1935          & Erd\H{o}s, Szekeres                                & $r(s,t) = O(t^{s-1})$ for all fixed $s \ge 3$ and $t \to \infty$.                                                                                      \\
        Upper Bound Impr.    & 1980          & Ajtai, Koml\'os, Szemer\'edi                       & First improvement to the upper bound on $r(s,t)$ by analyzing a randomized greedy algorithm for producing large independent sets.                      \\
        Lower Bound          & 2010          & Bohman, Keevash                                    & Lower bound by analyzing the random $K_s$-free graph process, improving on earlier results of Spencer.                                                 \\
        Upper Bound Impr.    & 2001          & Li, Rousseau, Zang                                 & Extending ideas of Shearer, showed that $r(s,t) \le (1+o(1))\frac{t^{s-1}}{(\log t)^{s-2}}$ as $t \to \infty$.                                         \\
        Lower Bound Brkthr.  & 1995          & Kim                                                & $r(3,t) = \Omega(t^2/\log t)$, matching previous upper bounds by Ajtai, Koml\'os and Szemer\'edi and Shearer, and improving earlier bounds of Spencer. \\
        Asymptotic Result    & 2020          & Fiz Pontiveros, Griffiths, Morris; Bohman, Keevash & $r(3,t)$ is determined asymptotically up to a factor of four.                                                                                          \\
        \bottomrule
    \end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\chapter{Lecture 4}

\section{Ramsey Theory on Structures and Spaces}

\begin{theorem}[Schur's Theorem]
    Let $n$ be a positive integer, and let $s_n$ be the smallest integer such that every
    $n$-coloring of $[s_n]=\{1,2,\dots,s_n\}$ contains a monochromatic solution to
    \[
        x+y=z.
    \]
    Then such an integer $s_n$ exists.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Identify the set of $n$ colors with $[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}$, and let
    \[
        N:=R(\underbrace{3,\dots,3}_{n\text{ times}})
    \]
    be the $n$-color Ramsey number for triangles.

    We claim that every coloring of $[N]$ with $n$ colors contains a monochromatic
    solution to $x+y=z$. This will imply that the set of positive integers with the
    required property is nonempty, and hence, by well-ordering, its least element
    $s_n$ exists.

    So let $c:[N]\to [n]$ be any coloring. Consider the complete graph $K_N$ with
    vertex set $[N]$. Color each edge $\{u,v\}$, where $u\neq v$, by the color
    \[
        c(|u-v|).
    \]
    Since $N=R(3,\dots,3)$, Ramsey's theorem guarantees a monochromatic triangle in
    this edge-colored complete graph. Thus there exist distinct vertices
    \[
        u,v,w\in [N]
    \]
    such that the three edges $\{u,v\}$, $\{u,w\}$, and $\{v,w\}$ all have the same
    color.

    Without loss of generality, assume
    \[
        u>v>w.
    \]
    Set
    \[
        x=u-v,\qquad y=v-w,\qquad z=u-w.
    \]
    Then $x,y,z\in [N]$, and
    \[
        x+y=(u-v)+(v-w)=u-w=z.
    \]
    Moreover, the numbers $x,y,z$ all receive the same color under $c$, because
    \[
        c(x)=c(|u-v|),\qquad c(y)=c(|v-w|),\qquad c(z)=c(|u-w|),
    \]
    and these three edge colors are equal by construction of the monochromatic triangle.

    Hence we have found a monochromatic solution to $x+y=z$. Therefore every
    $n$-coloring of $[N]$ contains such a solution, so the desired integer exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    Define $[q]^n:=\{(x_1,\cdots,x_n):x_i\in [q]\}$. We say $L\subseteq[q]^n$ is a \emph{combinatorial line} if there exists $I\subseteq [n]$ and integers $a_i$ such that
    $$
        L=\{(x_1,\cdots,x_n)\in [q]^n:x_i=a_i\text{ for }i\notin I\text{ and }x_i=x_j\text{ for all }i,j\in I\}.
    $$
\end{definition}


\begin{example}
    Consider $[3]^3=\{(x_1,x_2,x_3):x_i\in\{1,2,3\}\}$.
    Let
    \[
        I=\{1,2\},
        \qquad
        a_3=2.
    \]
    Then the set
    \[
        L=\{(x_1,x_2,x_3)\in [3]^3 : x_3=2 \text{ and } x_1=x_2\}
    \]
    is a combinatorial line. Explicitly,
    \[
        L=\{(1,1,2),(2,2,2),(3,3,2)\}.
    \]

    Indeed, the third coordinate is fixed, while the first two coordinates vary together and always take the same value.

    \medskip

    A schematic picture is given below. Since the three points lie in the plane $x_3=2$, we draw that slice as a $3\times 3$ grid and highlight the diagonal corresponding to the line $x_1=x_2$.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.2]

            % grid
            \draw[step=1, gray!50, thin] (1,1) grid (3,3);

            % points in the slice x_3=2
            \foreach \x in {1,2,3}{
                    \foreach \y in {1,2,3}{
                            \fill (\x,\y) circle (2pt);
                        }
                }

            % highlighted combinatorial line
            \draw[red, thick] (1,1) -- (3,3);
            \foreach \p in {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3)}{
                    \fill[red] \p circle (3pt);
                }

            % labels for highlighted points
            \node[below left]  at (1,1) {\small $(1,1,2)$};
            \node[above]       at (2,2) {\small $(2,2,2)$};
            \node[above right] at (3,3) {\small $(3,3,2)$};

            % axis labels
            \node[left]  at (0.7,1) {\small $x_2=1$};
            \node[left]  at (0.7,2) {\small $x_2=2$};
            \node[left]  at (0.7,3) {\small $x_2=3$};

            \node[below] at (1,0.7) {\small $x_1=1$};
            \node[below] at (2,0.7) {\small $x_1=2$};
            \node[below] at (3,0.7) {\small $x_1=3$};

            % slice label
            \node at (2,3.6) {\small slice $x_3=2$};

        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Hales--Jewett]
    For all $r,q\in \mathbb{N}_+$, there exists a smallest $n:=HJ(r,q)$ such that any $r$-coloring of $[q]^n$ contains a monochromatic combinatorial line.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    The proof proceeds by induction on $q$. The case $q=1$ is trivial (every point is a monochromatic line), so we assume $q\geqslant 2$ and that $HJ(r,q')$ is finite for all $q'\leqslant q-1$.

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 0: Key definitions.}
    For a combinatorial line $L$ in $[q]^n$, write $L^{-}$ for its \emph{first point} (all active coordinates equal $1$) and $L^+$ for its \emph{last point} (all active coordinates equal $q$).

    We say that lines $L_1,\cdots,L_s$ are \emph{focused at} $f$ if $L_i^+=f$ for every $i\in [s]$.
    They are \emph{color-focused at} $f$ (with respect to a coloring $\chi$) if, additionally, each truncated line $L_i\setminus\{L_i^+\}$ is monochromatic and these $s$ colors are pairwise different.

    The following picture illustrates 2 lines color-focused at the common endpoint $f$ (with $q=3$, so each line has 3 points and the truncation removes the last one):
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1, >=stealth]
            \fill[blue] (0,0) circle (3pt) node[below=4pt]{\small $L_1^-$};
            \fill[blue] (1.5,0) circle (3pt);
            \fill[black] (3,0.75) circle (4pt) node[right=4pt]{\small $f$};
            \fill[red]  (0,1.5) circle (3pt) node[above=4pt]{\small $L_2^-$};
            \fill[red]  (1.5,1.5) circle (3pt);
            \draw[->, blue, thick] (0,0) -- (1.5,0) -- (3,0.75);
            \draw[->, red, thick]  (0,1.5) -- (1.5,1.5) -- (3,0.75);
            \draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=5pt, mirror}] (-0.1,-0.4) -- (1.6,-0.4)
            node[midway, below=6pt]{\small \color{blue} mono blue};
            \draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=5pt}] (-0.1,1.9) -- (1.6,1.9)
            node[midway, above=6pt]{\small \color{red} mono red};
            \node at (1.5,-1.3) {\small $L_1\setminus\{f\}$ and $L_2\setminus\{f\}$: different colors};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 1: Reduction to a ``focused'' statement.}
    We prove the following strengthening by induction on $s$: for each $s\leqslant r$, there exists $N=\mathrm{FHJ}(r,s,q)$ such that any $r$-coloring of $[q]^N$ either
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item[(a)] contains a monochromatic combinatorial line, or
        \item[(b)] contains $s$ color-focused lines at some common point.
    \end{enumerate}
    Taking $s=r$: in case~(b), the $r$ truncated lines use $r$ pairwise different colors from a palette of $r$ colors, so all $r$ colors appear. At the common focus point $f$, the color of $f$ must equal one of them, yielding a monochromatic line. Thus case~(b) forces case~(a).

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 2: Base case ($s=1$).}
    Set $\mathrm{FHJ}(r,1,q)=HJ(r,q-1)$. By induction on $q$, any $r$-coloring of $[q-1]^{HJ(r,q-1)}$ contains a monochromatic line $L$ (using values in $[q-1]$). This $L$ already lies inside $[q]^{HJ(r,q-1)}$, and the truncation $L\setminus\{L^+\}$ is monochromatic, so $\{L\}$ is a single color-focused line at $L^+$.

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 3: Inductive step.}
    Suppose $\mathrm{FHJ}(r,s',q)$ is finite for all $s'\leqslant s-1$. Set
    \[
        n:=\mathrm{FHJ}(r,s-1,q), \qquad n':=HJ(r^{q^n},q-1), \qquad N:=n+n'.
    \]
    We show $\mathrm{FHJ}(r,s,q)\leqslant N$. The argument has three sub-steps, illustrated schematically below.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, >=stealth]
            \draw[thick, fill=blue!8] (0,0) rectangle (4,3);
            \node at (2,3.3) {\small $[q]^n$};
            \node at (2,1.5) {\small first $n$ coords};
            \draw[thick, fill=red!8] (5,0) rectangle (9.5,3);
            \node at (7.25,3.3) {\small $[q]^{n'}$};
            \node at (7.25,1.5) {\small last $n'$ coords};
            \draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=8pt, mirror}] (0,-.3) -- (9.5,-.3)
            node[midway, below=10pt]{\small $[q]^N = [q]^n \times [q]^{n'}$};
            \node[align=center] at (2,-2) {\footnotesize dimension chosen by\\\footnotesize $\mathrm{FHJ}(r,s-1,q)$};
            \node[align=center] at (7.25,-2) {\footnotesize dimension chosen by\\\footnotesize $HJ(r^{q^n},q-1)$};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 3a: Splitting and super-coloring.}
    Given an $r$-coloring $\chi$ of $[q]^N$, write each point as $(a,b)$ where $a\in [q]^n$ (first $n$ coordinates) and $b\in [q]^{n'}$ (last $n'$ coordinates). Define a \emph{super-coloring} $\chi'$ on $[q]^{n'}$ by assigning to each $b$ the entire function
    \[
        \chi'(b) := f_b, \qquad\text{where}\quad f_b\colon [q]^n\to [r],\quad a\mapsto \chi(a,b).
    \]
    There are at most $r^{q^n}$ possible such functions, so $\chi'$ is an $r^{q^n}$-coloring.

    \smallskip\noindent
    \textit{Intuition.} Two points $b,b'$ in $[q]^{n'}$ receive the same super-color if and only if the ``slices'' $[q]^n\times\{b\}$ and $[q]^n\times\{b'\}$ are colored identically under $\chi$.

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 3b: Applying Hales--Jewett in the second block.}
    Since $n'=HJ(r^{q^n},q-1)$, the super-coloring $\chi'$ on $[q-1]^{n'}\subseteq [q]^{n'}$ contains a monochromatic combinatorial line $L$ with active coordinates $I'\subseteq \{n{+}1,\cdots,n{+}n'\}$. ``Monochromatic under $\chi'$'' on $L\setminus\{L^+\}$ means:
    \[
        \forall\, b,b'\in L\setminus\{L^+\}:\quad f_b = f_{b'} \quad\text{(identical coloring on $[q]^n$)}.
    \]
    So we may define a \emph{well-defined} $r$-coloring on $[q]^n$:
    \[
        \chi''(a) := \chi(a,b) \quad\text{for any } b\in L\setminus\{L^+\}.
    \]

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85, >=stealth]
            \foreach \yy/\blab/\col in {0/{$b_1=L^-$}/green!70!black, 2/{$b_2$}/green!70!black, 4/{$b_3=L^+$}/gray}{
            \draw[thick, fill=\col!10] (0,\yy) rectangle (3.5,\yy+1.2);
            \node[left] at (-0.1,\yy+0.6) {\small \blab};
            }
            \node at (1.75,0.6)  {\small slice $\chi(\cdot,b_1)$};
            \node at (1.75,2.6) {\small slice $\chi(\cdot,b_2)$};
            \node at (1.75,4.6) {\small slice $\chi(\cdot,b_3)$};
            \draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=5pt, mirror}] (3.8,0) -- (3.8,3.2)
            node[midway, right=6pt, align=left] {\small same coloring\\\small $\Rightarrow$ defines $\chi''$};
            \draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=5pt}] (-2.5,0) -- (-2.5,5.2)
            node[midway, left=6pt] {\small line $L$ in $[q]^{n'}$};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 3c: Applying the focused induction in the first block.}
    Since $n=\mathrm{FHJ}(r,s-1,q)$, the coloring $\chi''$ on $[q]^n$ either
    \begin{itemize}
        \item contains a monochromatic line (done!), or
        \item contains $s-1$ lines $L_1,\cdots,L_{s-1}$ in $[q]^n$ that are color-focused at some point $f$.
    \end{itemize}
    Assume the latter. We now ``lift'' these $s-1$ lines to $[q]^N$ and add one more line to get $s$ color-focused lines.

    \medskip
    \noindent\textbf{Step 4: Constructing $s$ color-focused lines in $[q]^N$.}
    Write $I_i\subseteq\{1,\cdots,n\}$ for the active coordinates of $L_i$ in $[q]^n$. Define $s$ lines in $[q]^N$:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item For $1\leqslant i\leqslant s-1$: let $L_i'$ be the line with first point $(L_i^-,\, L^-)$ and active coordinates $I_i\cup I'$.
        \item For $i=s$: let $L_s'$ be the line with first point $(f,\, L^-)$ and active coordinates $I'$ only.
    \end{itemize}

    We illustrate with $q=3$, $s=3$ (so $s{-}1=2$ old lines plus one new line). Each line has $3$ points; writing them from first ($t=1$) to last ($t=3$):

    \smallskip
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, every node/.style={font=\small},
                pt/.style={circle, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=5pt, fill=#1},
                pt/.default=black]

            %% ============ LEFT PANEL: ingredients ============
            \node[font=\normalsize\bfseries] at (2.2,5.8) {Ingredients};

            % -- Lines L_1, L_2 in [q]^n, color-focused at f --
            \node[anchor=east] at (-0.3,4.5) {$[q]^n$:};

            % L_1 (blue): 3 points
            \node[pt=blue] (L1a) at (0.5,4.5) {};
            \node[pt=blue] (L1b) at (1.8,4.5) {};
            \node[pt=violet] (f1) at (3.1,4.5) {};
            \draw[blue, thick] (L1a) -- (L1b) -- (f1);
            \node[blue, above] at (0.5,4.5) {$L_1^-$};
            \node[violet,above] at (3.1,4.65) {$f$};
            \node[blue, below=2pt] at (1.2,4.5) {$L_1 \setminus \{L_1^+\}$ \color{blue!70!black}(mono blue)};

            % L_2 (red): 3 points
            \node[pt=red] (L2a) at (0.5,3.2) {};
            \node[pt=red] (L2b) at (1.8,3.2) {};
            \node[pt=violet] (f2) at (3.1,3.2) {};
            \draw[red, thick] (L2a) -- (L2b) -- (f2);
            \node[red, above] at (0.5,3.2) {$L_2^-$};
            \node[violet, above] at (3.1,3.35) {$f$};
            \node[red, below=2pt] at (1.2,3.2) {$L_2 \setminus \{L_2^+\}$ \color{red!70!black}(mono red)};

            % -- Line L in [q]^{n'} --
            \node[anchor=east] at (-0.3,1.5) {$[q]^{n'}$:};

            \node[pt=green!60!black] (La) at (0.5,1.5) {};
            \node[pt=green!60!black] (Lb) at (1.8,1.5) {};
            \node[pt=black!60!black] (Lc) at (3.1,1.5) {};
            \draw[green!60!black, thick] (La) -- (Lb) -- (Lc);
            \node[green!60!black, above] at (0.5,1.5) {$L^-$};
            \node[black!60!black, above] at (3.1,1.5) {$L^+$};
            \node[green!60!black, below=2pt] at (1.8,1.5) {$L$ (mono under $\chi'$ on $L\setminus\{L^+\}$)};

            %% ============ ARROW ============
            \draw[->, very thick, gray] (4.5,3.5) -- node[above]{\normalsize lift} (5,3.5);

            %% ============ RIGHT PANEL: lifted lines in [q]^N ============
            \node[font=\normalsize\bfseries] at (9,5.8) {Lifted lines in $[q]^N$};

            % Column headers: t=1, t=2, t=3
            \node at (6.5,5.2) {$t=1$};
            \node at (8.5,5.2) {$t=2$};
            \node at (10.5,5.2) {$t=3$};

            % L'_1 (blue): active in I_1 ∪ I'
            % t=1: (L_1^-,L^-), t=2: (mid,mid), t=3: (f,L^+)
            \node[pt=blue] (L1p1) at (6.5,4.5) {};
            \node[pt=blue] (L1p2) at (8.5,4.5) {};
            \node[pt=black] (L1p3) at (10.5,4.5) {};
            \draw[blue, thick] (L1p1) -- (L1p2) -- (L1p3);
            \node[blue, above=1pt] at (6.5,4.5) {\footnotesize $(L_1^-,L^-)$};
            \node[blue, left=2pt, align=right] at (6.2,4.5) {$L_1'$};

            % L'_2 (red): active in I_2 ∪ I'
            \node[pt=red] (L2p1) at (6.5,3.2) {};
            \node[pt=red] (L2p2) at (8.5,3.2) {};
            \node[pt=black] (L2p3) at (10.5,3.2) {};
            \draw[red, thick] (L2p1) -- (L2p2) -- (L2p3);
            \node[red, above=1pt] at (6.5,3.2) {\footnotesize $(L_2^-,L^-)$};
            \node[red, left=2pt, align=right] at (6.2,3.2) {$L_2'$};

            % L'_s (violet): active in I' only
            \node[pt=violet] (Lsp1) at (6.5,1.9) {};
            \node[pt=violet] (Lsp2) at (8.5,1.9) {};
            \node[pt=black] (Lsp3) at (10.5,1.9) {};
            \draw[violet, thick] (Lsp1) -- (Lsp2) -- (Lsp3);
            \node[violet, above=1pt] at (6.5,1.9) {\footnotesize $(f,L^-)$};
            \node[violet, left=2pt, align=right] at (6.2,1.9) {$L_s'$};

            % common focus
            \draw[thick, densely dotted] (10.5,4.5) -- (10.5,1.9);
            \node[right=3pt, align=left] at (10.8,3.2) {\textbf{$(f,L^+)$}\\common\\focus};

            % braces for active coords
            \draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=4pt, mirror}] (6.3,3.0) -- (10.7,3.0)
            node[midway, below=5pt] {\footnotesize active in $I_1\cup I'$: both $a,b$ vary};
            \draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=4pt, mirror}] (6.3,1.6) -- (10.7,1.6)
            node[midway, below=5pt] {\footnotesize active in $I'$ only: $a=f$ fixed, only $b$ varies};

        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    \noindent\textbf{Verification.}
    All $s$ lines share the common last point $(f,L^+)$, so they are focused there. It remains to check that the truncations use pairwise different colors:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item For $1\leqslant i\leqslant s-1$: the points of $L_i'\setminus\{(f,L^+)\}$ have the form $(x,b)$ where $x$ ranges over $L_i\setminus\{f\}$ and $b$ ranges over $L\setminus\{L^+\}$. Since $f_b=f_{b'}$ for all $b,b'\in L\setminus\{L^+\}$, the color $\chi(x,b)=\chi''(x)$, which is the monochromatic color of $L_i\setminus\{f\}$ under $\chi''$.
        \item For $i=s$: the points of $L_s'\setminus\{(f,L^+)\}$ have the form $(f,b)$ where $b\in L\setminus\{L^+\}$. Their color is $\chi(f,b)=\chi''(f)$, which is the color of the focus $f$ under $\chi''$.
    \end{itemize}
    Since $L_1,\cdots,L_{s-1}$ were color-focused at $f$ under $\chi''$, their $s-1$ truncation colors are pairwise different and all differ from $\chi''(f)$, giving $s$ pairwise different colors in total. Hence $L_1',\cdots,L_s'$ are color-focused at $(f,L^+)$ under $\chi$.
\end{proof}

\section{Tur\'an-type Problems I}

\begin{definition}
    Let $n$ be an integer, and let $H$ be a graph. The \emph{Tur\'an number} $\mathrm{ex}(n,H)$ denotes the maximal number of edges in an $H$-free graph on $n$ vertices. Here $H$-free means $H$ is not a subgraph.

    More generally, define $\mathrm{ex}(n,\{H_1,\cdots,H_k\})$ for all $H_i$ forbidden.
\end{definition}


\begin{lemma}
    $\mathrm{ex}(n,\{C_3,C_4,\cdots,C_n\})=n-1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proposition}[Mantel, 1907]
    $\mathrm{ex}(n,C_3)=\lfloor n^2/4\rfloor$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof 1]
    Let $G=(V,E)$ be $C_3$-free. Define $n=|V|$ and $m=|E|$. For $\{x,y\}\in E$, as $G$ is $C_3$-free, $d(x)+d(y)\leqslant n$. By double-counting walks of length $2$,
    \[
        \sum_{x\in V}d(x)^2=\sum_{\{x,y\}\in E}(d(x)+d(y))\leqslant mn.
    \]
    By Cauchy--Schwarz and $\sum d(x)=2m$,
    \[
        mn\geqslant\sum_{x\in V}d(x)^2\geqslant\frac{\left(\sum d(x)\right)^2}{n}=\frac{4m^2}{n}.
    \]
    Hence $m\leqslant n^2/4$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof 2]
    Let $A$ be the largest coclique (i.e.\ independent set) of $G$. As $N(x)$ is edge-less, $|A|\geqslant |N(x)| =d(x)$ for all $x\in V$. Also, as $A$ is largest, $B:=V\setminus A$ meets every edge. Hence $\sum_{x\in B}d(x)\geqslant |E|$. Therefore
    \[
        |E|\leqslant \sum_{x\in B}d(x)\leqslant |B|\cdot |A|\leqslant\left(\frac{|A|+|B|}{2}\right)^2=\frac{n^2}{4}.
    \]
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof 3]
    We prove by induction on $m:=|V(G)|$ that every triangle-free graph $G$ on $m$ vertices satisfies
    \[
        |E(G)|\le \left\lfloor \frac{m^{2}}{4}\right\rfloor .
    \]

    If $m\le 2$, then clearly $|E(G)|\le 1$, so the claim is immediate.

    Now assume $m\ge 3$ and that the statement holds for all triangle-free graphs on $m-2$ vertices. Let $G$ be a triangle-free graph on $m$ vertices.

    If $E(G)=\varnothing$, then there is nothing to prove. So choose an edge $xy\in E(G)$, and let
    \[
        H:=G[V(G)\setminus\{x,y\}]
    \]
    be the induced subgraph obtained by deleting $x$ and $y$. Then $H$ is triangle-free and has $m-2$ vertices. Hence, by the induction hypothesis,
    \[
        |E(H)|\le \left\lfloor \frac{(m-2)^2}{4}\right\rfloor .
    \]

    Since $G$ is triangle-free and $xy\in E(G)$, the vertices $x$ and $y$ have no common neighbor other than each other. Indeed, if some vertex $z\neq x,y$ were adjacent to both $x$ and $y$, then $x,y,z$ would form a triangle. Therefore
    \[
        \bigl(N(x)\setminus\{y\}\bigr)\cap \bigl(N(y)\setminus\{x\}\bigr)=\varnothing.
    \]
    Both sets are contained in $V(G)\setminus\{x,y\}$, which has size $m-2$. Thus
    \[
        (d(x)-1)+(d(y)-1)\le m-2,
    \]
    and hence
    \[
        d(x)+d(y)\le m.
    \]

    Every edge of $G$ is either an edge of $H$, or is incident with $x$ or $y$. Therefore
    \[
        |E(G)|=|E(H)|+d(x)+d(y)-1,
    \]
    where we subtract $1$ because the edge $xy$ is counted in both $d(x)$ and $d(y)$. It follows that
    \[
        |E(G)|
        \le
        \left\lfloor \frac{(m-2)^2}{4}\right\rfloor + m - 1.
    \]

    It remains to check that
    \[
        \left\lfloor \frac{(m-2)^2}{4}\right\rfloor + m - 1
        =
        \left\lfloor \frac{m^2}{4}\right\rfloor .
    \]
    If $m=2n$, then
    \[
        \left\lfloor \frac{(m-2)^2}{4}\right\rfloor + m - 1
        =
        \frac{(2n-2)^2}{4}+2n-1
        =
        (n-1)^2+2n-1
        =
        n^2
        =
        \left\lfloor \frac{(2n)^2}{4}\right\rfloor .
    \]
    If $m=2n+1$, then
    \[
        \left\lfloor \frac{(m-2)^2}{4}\right\rfloor + m - 1
        =
        \left\lfloor \frac{(2n-1)^2}{4}\right\rfloor +2n
        =
        (n^2-n)+2n
        =
        n^2+n
        =
        \left\lfloor \frac{(2n+1)^2}{4}\right\rfloor .
    \]
    Thus in all cases,
    \[
        |E(G)|\le \left\lfloor \frac{m^2}{4}\right\rfloor .
    \]

    Therefore
    \[
        \mathrm{ex}(n,C_3)=\left\lfloor \frac{n^2}{4}\right\rfloor .
    \]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Tur\'an, 1941]
    For every integer $k \ge 2$,
    \[
        \operatorname{ex}(n,K_{k+1})
        =
        \left\lfloor \left(1-\frac1k\right)\frac{n^2}{2}\right\rfloor .
    \]
    Moreover, equality holds if and only if the graph is the complete $k$-partite graph
    whose part sizes differ by at most $1$, i.e.\ the Tur\'an graph
    \[
        T_{n,k}=K_{\lceil n/k\rceil,\ldots,\lfloor n/k\rfloor}.
    \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Write
    \[
        t_{n,k}:=\left\lfloor \left(1-\frac1k\right)\frac{n^2}{2}\right\rfloor .
    \]
    We prove simultaneously the upper bound and the uniqueness statement by induction on $n$,
    with a subsidiary induction on $k$.

    The statement is trivial when $n\le k$, since then every graph on $n$ vertices is
    $K_{k+1}$-free and
    \[
        \operatorname{ex}(n,K_{k+1})=\binom{n}{2}=t_{n,k}.
    \]
    The case $k=2$ is Mantel's theorem.

    Now let $k\ge 3$ and $n\ge k+1$, and assume the theorem holds for all smaller values of
    $n$, and also for the parameter $k-1$.

    Let $G$ be a $K_{k+1}$-free graph on $n$ vertices.

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{Step 1: the upper bound.}
    If $G$ contains no copy of $K_k$, then $G$ is $K_k$-free, so by the induction
    hypothesis on $k-1$,
    \[
        e(G)\le t_{n,k-1}<t_{n,k},
    \]
    and hence in this case there is nothing more to prove.

    Therefore we may assume that $G$ contains a $k$-clique.
    Choose such a clique
    \[
        A=\{a_1,\dots,a_k\},
        \qquad
        B:=V(G)\setminus A.
    \]
    Then
    \[
        e(G)=e(A)+e(B)+e(A,B).
    \]
    Since $A$ is a $k$-clique,
    \[
        e(A)=\binom{k}{2}.
    \]
    Also, the induced subgraph $G[B]$ is still $K_{k+1}$-free, so by the induction
    hypothesis on $n-k<n$,
    \[
        e(B)\le t_{n-k,k}.
    \]
    Finally, every vertex $b\in B$ is adjacent to at most $k-1$ vertices of $A$,
    for otherwise $A\cup\{b\}$ would span a $K_{k+1}$.
    Hence
    \[
        e(A,B)\le (k-1)(n-k).
    \]
    Combining these estimates gives
    \[
        e(G)
        \le
        \binom{k}{2}+t_{n-k,k}+(k-1)(n-k).
    \]
    Since the first and third terms are integers,
    \[
        \binom{k}{2}+t_{n-k,k}+(k-1)(n-k)
        \le
        \left\lfloor
        \binom{k}{2}
        +\left(1-\frac1k\right)\frac{(n-k)^2}{2}
        +(k-1)(n-k)
        \right\rfloor.
    \]
    A direct calculation shows that
    \[
        \binom{k}{2}
        +\left(1-\frac1k\right)\frac{(n-k)^2}{2}
        +(k-1)(n-k)
        =
        \left(1-\frac1k\right)\frac{n^2}{2},
    \]
    therefore
    \[
        e(G)\le t_{n,k}.
    \]
    Thus
    \[
        \operatorname{ex}(n,K_{k+1})\le t_{n,k}.
    \]

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{Step 2: the extremal construction.}
    The complete $k$-partite graph $T_{n,k}$ has no $(k+1)$-clique, since any clique in a
    $k$-partite graph contains at most one vertex from each part.
    Its number of edges is
    \[
        e(T_{n,k})=t_{n,k}.
    \]
    Hence
    \[
        \operatorname{ex}(n,K_{k+1})\ge t_{n,k}.
    \]
    Together with the upper bound, this yields
    \[
        \operatorname{ex}(n,K_{k+1})=t_{n,k}.
    \]

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{Step 3: the equality case.}
    Suppose now that $e(G)=t_{n,k}$.

    As observed above, $G$ must contain a $K_k$, for otherwise
    $e(G)\le t_{n,k-1}<t_{n,k}$.
    Fix a $k$-clique
    \[
        A=\{a_1,\dots,a_k\},
        \qquad
        B:=V(G)\setminus A.
    \]
    Since equality holds in
    \[
        e(G)\le \binom{k}{2}+e(B)+e(A,B)\le \binom{k}{2}+t_{n-k,k}+(k-1)(n-k),
    \]
    we must have equality in each of the intermediate estimates. In particular:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item[(i)] $e(B)=t_{n-k,k}$, so by the induction hypothesis on $n$, the graph $G[B]$
              is the Tur\'an graph $T_{n-k,k}$;
        \item[(ii)] every vertex $b\in B$ is adjacent to exactly $k-1$ vertices of $A$.
    \end{enumerate}

    By (ii), for each $b\in B$ there is a unique index $i(b)\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ such that
    $b$ is \emph{not} adjacent to $a_{i(b)}$.
    For each $i\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ define
    \[
        C_i:=\{a_i\}\cup \{\, b\in B : i(b)=i \,\}.
    \]
    Then the sets $C_1,\dots,C_k$ form a partition of $V(G)$.

    We claim that each $C_i$ is an independent set.
    Indeed, by definition $a_i$ is adjacent to no vertex of $C_i\setminus\{a_i\}$.
    If $x,y\in C_i\setminus\{a_i\}$ were adjacent, then both $x$ and $y$ would be adjacent
    to every vertex of $A\setminus\{a_i\}$; hence
    \[
        \{x,y\}\cup (A\setminus\{a_i\})
    \]
    would span a $K_{k+1}$, a contradiction.
    Thus each $C_i$ is independent.

    Therefore $G$ is a spanning subgraph of the complete $k$-partite graph with
    $C_1,\dots,C_k$ parts, and so
    \[
        e(G)\le \sum_{1\le i<j\le k} |C_i||C_j|.
    \]
    For fixed $\sum_i |C_i|=n$, the quantity on the right is maximized exactly when the part
    sizes differ by at most $1$, and its maximum is $t_{n,k}$.
    Since $e(G)=t_{n,k}$, we obtain
    \[
        t_{n,k}=e(G)\le \sum_{i<j}|C_i||C_j|\le t_{n,k}.
    \]
    Hence equality holds throughout. It follows that:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item the sizes $|C_1|,\dots,|C_k|$ differ by at most $1$;
        \item all edges between distinct parts $C_i$ and $C_j$ are present.
    \end{itemize}
    Thus $G$ is exactly the complete $k$-partite graph with part sizes as equal as possible,
    namely
    \[
        G\cong T_{n,k}=K_{\lceil n/k\rceil,\ldots,\lfloor n/k\rfloor}.
    \]

    Conversely, $T_{n,k}$ is $K_{k+1}$-free and has $t_{n,k}$ edges, so equality indeed holds
    for this graph.

    This completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Lecture 5}

\section{Tur\'an-type Problems II}

Recall Jensen's inequality:
\[
    \phi\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\right) \leq \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \phi(x_i).
\]
where $\phi$ is a convex function $\phi: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$.

\begin{theorem}
    $\operatorname{ex}(n,\{C_3,C_4\})\leqslant n\sqrt{n-1}/2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Define $n=|V(G)|$ and $m=|E(G)|$.
    We count paths $(x,y,z)$, where $x,y,z\in V(G)$.

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{Count 1:} First pick $x$ and $z$, and there are $n(n-1)$ ways.
    If $x\sim z$, then $0$ choice for $y$; if $x\nsim z$, then at most $1$ choice for $y$.
    Hence the number of paths $\leqslant n(n-1)-2m$.

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{Count 2:} First pick $y$, then $x$ and $z$.
    By Jensen's inequality, we have
    \[
        n(n-1)-2m\geqslant\#\text{paths}=\sum_{y\in V(G)}\deg(y)(\deg(y)-1)\geqslant n\cdot\frac{2m}{n}\left(\frac{2m}{n}-1\right).
    \]

    It yields that $m\leqslant n\sqrt{n-1}/2$ and we finish the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\leavevmode
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Equality holds when:
              \begin{itemize}
                  \item $n=2$, $K_2$;
                  \item $n=5$, pentagon;
                  \item $n=10$, Petersen graph;
                  \item $n=50$, Hoffman--Singleton graph;
                  \item $n=3250$, open case.
              \end{itemize}
        \item Asymptotic: $\operatorname{ex}(n,\{C_3,C_4\})=(\frac{1}{2}+o(1))n^{3/2}$. Construction uses polarities of projective planes.
        \item $\operatorname{ex}(n,C_4)=\bigl(\frac{1}{2}+o(1)\bigr)n^{3/2}$。
    \end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
    Consider $\operatorname{ex}(n,\{C_3,\ldots,C_{2m}\})$.
    Classical lower-bound constructions come from incidence graphs of generalized $(m+1)$-gons:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $m=2$: projective planes (generalized triangles);
        \item $m=3$: generalized quadrangles;
        \item $m=5$: generalized hexagons.
    \end{itemize}
    Indeed, the incidence graph of a generalized $d$-gon is bipartite of girth $2d$ and diameter $d$,
    so it contains no cycle of length less than $2d$, and hence gives a construction for
    $\operatorname{ex}(n,\{C_3,\ldots,C_{2d-2}\})$.
\end{remark}

\section{Hall's Marriage Theorem}

\begin{definition}
    Let $G=(S\cup T,E)$ be a bipartite graph.
    A \textbf{matching} $M\subseteq E$ of $G$ is a set of pairwise disjoint edges.
    Define $m(G)=\max\limits_{M\text{ matching}}|M|$, and we say $M$ is a \textbf{maximum matching} if $|M|=m(G)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    A maximal matching may not be maximum, just like maximal subgroups of a group have different orders.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Hall's Marriage Theorem]\label{thm:hall}
    Let $G=(S\cup T,E)$ be a bipartite graph.
    Then $m(G)=|S|$ if and only if $|A|\leqslant |N(A)|$ for all $A\subseteq S$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    The ``only if'' direction is immediate: if there is a matching saturating $S$,
    then for every subset $A \subseteq S$, the vertices of $A$ are matched to
    distinct vertices in $N(A)$, so necessarily
    \[
        |A| \le |N(A)|.
    \]

    We now prove the converse. Assume that
    \[
        |A| \le |N(A)| \qquad \text{for every } A \subseteq S.
    \]
    We shall show that there exists a matching saturating $S$.

    Choose a matching $M$ of maximum size. Suppose for a contradiction that
    $M$ does \emph{not} saturate $S$. Then there exists a vertex
    \[
        u_0 \in S
    \]
    which is unmatched by $M$.

    Starting from $u_0$, we construct sets
    \[
        U=\{u_0,u_1,u_2,\dots\}\subseteq S,
        \qquad
        V=\{v_1,v_2,\dots\}\subseteq T
    \]
    as follows.

    \medskip

    \noindent
    \textbf{Step 1.}
    Since $|N(\{u_0\})| \ge 1$, the vertex $u_0$ has some neighbor $v_1 \in T$.

    If $v_1$ is unmatched by $M$, then
    \[
        M' = M \cup \{\{u_0,v_1\}\}
    \]
    is a matching larger than $M$, contradicting the maximality of $M$.
    Hence $v_1$ must be matched in $M$, say
    \[
        \{u_1,v_1\}\in M
    \]
    for some $u_1\in S$.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                yscale=1.2,
                matched/.style={very thick, blue},
                unmatched/.style={thick, dashed, red!70!black},
                svert/.style={vertex, fill=black!80, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
                tvert/.style={vertex, fill=black!40, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
            ]
            \node[svert, label=left:{$u_0$}] (u0) at (0,2) {};
            \node[svert, label=left:{$u_1$}] (u1) at (0,1) {};
            \node[tvert, label=right:{$v_1$}] (v1) at (2.5,1.5) {};

            \draw[unmatched] (u0) -- node[above, font=\footnotesize] {$\notin M$} (v1);
            \draw[matched] (u1) -- node[below, font=\footnotesize] {$\in M$} (v1);

            \node[font=\footnotesize, anchor=north] at (0,0.4) {$S$};
            \node[font=\footnotesize, anchor=north] at (2.5,0.4) {$T$};

            \draw[gray, thin] (-0.4,0.5) rectangle (0.4,2.5);
            \draw[gray, thin] (2.1,0.5) rectangle (2.9,2.5);

            \node[red!70!black, font=\footnotesize] at (0,2.7) {unmatched};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    \medskip

    \noindent
    \textbf{Inductive step.}
    Suppose we have already found distinct vertices
    \[
        u_0,u_1,\dots,u_k \in S
        \quad\text{and}\quad
        v_1,\dots,v_k \in T
    \]
    such that for each $i=1,\dots,k$,
    \[
        \{u_i,v_i\}\in M,
    \]
    and every neighbor of $\{u_0,\dots,u_{k-1}\}$ that we have encountered so far
    lies in $\{v_1,\dots,v_k\}$.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                xscale=1.5, yscale=1.3,
                matched/.style={very thick, blue},
                unmatched/.style={thick, dashed, red!70!black},
                svert/.style={vertex, fill=black!80, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
                tvert/.style={vertex, fill=black!40, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
            ]
            \node[svert, label=left:{$u_0$}] (u0) at (0,3) {};
            \node[svert, label=left:{$u_1$}] (u1) at (0,2) {};
            \node[svert, label=left:{$u_2$}] (u2) at (0,1) {};
            \node at (0,0.3) {$\vdots$};
            \node[svert, label=left:{$u_k$}] (uk) at (0,-0.5) {};

            \node[tvert, label=right:{$v_1$}] (v1) at (2.5,2.5) {};
            \node[tvert, label=right:{$v_2$}] (v2) at (2.5,1.5) {};
            \node at (2.5,0.7) {$\vdots$};
            \node[tvert, label=right:{$v_k$}] (vk) at (2.5,-0.1) {};
            \node[tvert, label=right:{\color{red!70!black}$v_{k+1}$\,?}] (vk1) at (2.5,-1) {};

            \draw[unmatched] (u0) -- (v1);
            \draw[matched] (u1) -- (v1);
            \draw[unmatched] (u1) -- (v2);
            \draw[matched] (u2) -- (v2);
            \draw[matched] (uk) -- (vk);

            \draw[unmatched, thick, red!70!black] (uk) -- (vk1);
            \node[font=\scriptsize, red!70!black] at (1.25,-1.5) {new edge from $A_k$};

            \draw[gray, thin] (-0.6,-1.3) rectangle (0.6,3.5);
            \draw[gray, thin] (1.9,-1.5) rectangle (3.1,3.1);
            \node[font=\footnotesize, gray] at (0,3.8) {$A_k\subseteq S$};
            \node[font=\footnotesize, gray] at (2.5,3.4) {$N(A_k)\subseteq T$};

            \draw [decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=4pt}, gray]
            (3.3,3.1) -- (3.3,-0.1) node[midway, right=5pt, font=\scriptsize, gray] {$k$ vertices};
            \draw [decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=4pt, mirror}, gray]
            (-0.8,3.5) -- (-0.8,-0.8) node[midway, left=5pt, font=\scriptsize, gray] {$k{+}1$ vertices};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    Consider the set
    \[
        A_k=\{u_0,u_1,\dots,u_k\}\subseteq S.
    \]
    By Hall's condition,
    \[
        |N(A_k)| \ge |A_k| = k+1.
    \]
    But the set $\{v_1,\dots,v_k\}$ has only $k$ vertices, so $N(A_k)$ cannot be
    contained in $\{v_1,\dots,v_k\}$. Therefore there exists some
    \[
        v_{k+1}\in N(A_k)\setminus \{v_1,\dots,v_k\}.
    \]
    Choose $u_j \in A_k$ adjacent to $v_{k+1}$.

    If $v_{k+1}$ is unmatched by $M$, then we have found an augmenting path
    \[
        u_0,\, v_1,\, u_1,\, v_2,\, u_2,\, \dots,\, v_k,\, u_k,\, v_{k+1},
    \]
    where the edges
    \[
        u_i v_i \in M \quad (1\le i\le k)
    \]
    are matched edges, and the other edges are not in $M$.
    By exchanging matched and unmatched edges along this path, we obtain a new
    matching with one more edge than $M$, again contradicting the maximality of $M$.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                xscale=1.6, yscale=1.1,
                matched/.style={very thick, blue},
                unmatched/.style={thick, dashed, red!70!black},
                newmatched/.style={very thick, ForestGreen},
                svert/.style={vertex, fill=black!80, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
                tvert/.style={vertex, fill=black!40, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
            ]
            \node[svert, label=above:{$u_0$}] (u0) at (0,1) {};
            \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_1$}] (v1) at (1,1) {};
            \node[svert, label=above:{$u_1$}] (u1) at (2,1) {};
            \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_2$}] (v2) at (3,1) {};
            \node at (3.7,1) {$\cdots$};
            \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_k$}] (vk) at (4.4,1) {};
            \node[svert, label=above:{$u_k$}] (uk) at (5.4,1) {};
            \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_{k+1}$}] (vk1) at (6.4,1) {};

            \draw[unmatched] (u0) -- (v1);
            \draw[matched] (v1) -- (u1);
            \draw[unmatched] (u1) -- (v2);
            \draw[matched] (vk) -- (uk);
            \draw[unmatched] (uk) -- (vk1);

            \node[red!70!black, font=\scriptsize] at (0,0.4) {unmatched};
            \node[red!70!black, font=\scriptsize] at (6.4,0.4) {unmatched};

            \node[font=\small] at (3.2, 0) {Before: augmenting path};

            \draw[->, thick, gray] (3.2,-0.4) -- (3.2,-1.0);

            \begin{scope}[yshift=-2cm]
                \node[svert, label=above:{$u_0$}] (u0') at (0,1) {};
                \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_1$}] (v1') at (1,1) {};
                \node[svert, label=above:{$u_1$}] (u1') at (2,1) {};
                \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_2$}] (v2') at (3,1) {};
                \node at (3.7,1) {$\cdots$};
                \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_k$}] (vk') at (4.4,1) {};
                \node[svert, label=above:{$u_k$}] (uk') at (5.4,1) {};
                \node[tvert, label=above:{$v_{k+1}$}] (vk1') at (6.4,1) {};

                \draw[newmatched] (u0') -- (v1');
                \draw[unmatched, gray!60] (v1') -- (u1');
                \draw[newmatched] (u1') -- (v2');
                \draw[unmatched, gray!60] (vk') -- (uk');
                \draw[newmatched] (uk') -- (vk1');

                \node[font=\small] at (3.2, 0) {After: $|M'|=|M|+1$};
            \end{scope}

            \node[font=\scriptsize, blue, anchor=west] at (7.2,1) {$\in M$};
            \draw[matched] (6.9,1) -- (7.15,1);
            \node[font=\scriptsize, red!70!black, anchor=west] at (7.2,0.5) {$\notin M$};
            \draw[unmatched] (6.9,0.5) -- (7.15,0.5);
            \node[font=\scriptsize, ForestGreen, anchor=west] at (7.2,-1) {$\in M'$};
            \draw[newmatched] (6.9,-1) -- (7.15,-1);
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    So $v_{k+1}$ must be matched by $M$. Since $v_{k+1}\notin \{v_1,\dots,v_k\}$,
    its partner in $M$ cannot be any of $u_1,\dots,u_k$ already paired with
    $v_1,\dots,v_k$. Hence there exists a new vertex
    \[
        u_{k+1}\in S\setminus \{u_0,\dots,u_k\}
    \]
    such that
    \[
        \{u_{k+1},v_{k+1}\}\in M.
    \]
    This continues the construction.

    \medskip

    Thus, as long as no unmatched vertex of $T$ is reached, we obtain larger and
    larger sets
    \[
        A_k=\{u_0,\dots,u_k\}\subseteq S
    \]
    together with distinct vertices $v_1,\dots,v_{k+1}\in N(A_k)$.
    Since the graph is finite, this process cannot continue indefinitely. Therefore,
    at some stage we must encounter a vertex $v_{m+1}\in T$ that is unmatched by
    $M$.

    But then the alternating path
    \[
        u_0,\, v_1,\, u_1,\, v_2,\, \dots,\, v_m,\, u_m,\, v_{m+1}
    \]
    is an augmenting path for $M$. Replacing on this path the matched edges of $M$
    by the unmatched ones produces a matching with size $|M|+1$, contradicting
    the maximality of $M$.

    This contradiction shows that every maximum matching must in fact saturate $S$.
    Hence there exists a matching that matches every vertex of $S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    A \textbf{vertex cover} of a graph is a set $W\subseteq V$ such that each edge of $E$ contains at least one vertex of $W$.
\end{definition}

The following proposition is a generalization of Theorem~\ref{thm:hall}.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:deficiency}
    Let $G=(S\cup T,E)$ be a bipartite graph. Then
    \[
        m(G)=|S|-\max_{A\subseteq S}\bigl(|A|-|N(A)|\bigr).
    \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Set
    \[
        \delta:=\max_{A\subseteq S}\bigl(|A|-|N(A)|\bigr).
    \]
    Choose a subset $A_0\subseteq S$ such that
    \[
        |A_0|-|N(A_0)|=\delta.
    \]

    We first show that
    \[
        m(G)\le |S|-\delta.
    \]
    Indeed, let $M$ be any matching in $G$. Every vertex of $A_0$ matched by $M$
    must be matched to a distinct vertex of $N(A_0)$, since vertices of $A_0$ can
    only be matched to their neighbors. Therefore at most $|N(A_0)|$ vertices of
    $A_0$ can be matched. Hence at least
    \[
        |A_0|-|N(A_0)|=\delta
    \]
    vertices of $A_0$ remain unmatched. So every matching leaves at least $\delta$
    vertices of $S$ unmatched, and therefore
    \[
        |M|\le |S|-\delta.
    \]
    Since $M$ was arbitrary, it follows that
    \[
        m(G)\le |S|-\delta.
    \]

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                yscale=1.0, xscale=1.1,
                matched/.style={very thick, blue},
                svert/.style={vertex, fill=black!80, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
                tvert/.style={vertex, fill=black!40, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},
            ]

            \node[svert] (s2) at (0,3.3) {};

            \node[svert, fill=red!60] (a2) at (0,1.7) {};
            \node[svert, fill=red!60] (a3) at (0,1.0) {};
            \node[svert, fill=red!60] (a4) at (0,0.3) {};

            \node[tvert] (t1) at (3,3.8) {};
            \node[tvert] (t2) at (3,3.1) {};
            \node[tvert, fill=orange!50] (n1) at (3,2.1) {};
            \node[tvert, fill=orange!50] (n2) at (3,1.4) {};
            \node[tvert] (t3) at (3,0.5) {};

            \draw[matched] (s1) -- (t1);
            \draw[matched] (s2) -- (t2);
            \draw[matched] (a1) -- (n1);
            \draw[matched] (a2) -- (n2);

            \draw[gray, thin] (a1) -- (n1);
            \draw[gray, thin] (a2) -- (n2);
            \draw[gray, thin, dashed] (a3) -- (n1);
            \draw[gray, thin, dashed] (a3) -- (n2);
            \draw[gray, thin, dashed] (a4) -- (n1);

            \draw [decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=5pt, mirror}, red!70!black]
            (0.35,0.0) -- (0.35,2.7) node[midway, right=6pt, font=\scriptsize, red!70!black] {$A_0$};
            \draw [decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=5pt}, orange!70!black]
            (2.65,1.1) -- (2.65,2.4) node[midway, left=6pt, font=\scriptsize, orange!70!black] {$N(A_0)$};

            \node[font=\scriptsize, red!70!black] at (0,-0.4) {$|A_0|=4$};
            \node[font=\scriptsize, orange!70!black] at (3,-0.2) {$|N(A_0)|=2$};
            \node[font=\scriptsize] at (1.5,-0.9) {$\delta=|A_0|-|N(A_0)|=2$ vertices must remain unmatched};

            \draw[gray, thin, rounded corners] (-0.5,-0.1) rectangle (0.5,4.5);
            \draw[gray, thin, rounded corners] (2.5,0.1) rectangle (3.5,4.3);
            \node[font=\footnotesize, gray] at (0,4.8) {$S$};
            \node[font=\footnotesize, gray] at (3,4.6) {$T$};

            \node[font=\scriptsize, blue, anchor=west] at (4,4) {matched};
            \draw[matched] (3.7,4) -- (3.95,4);
            \node[font=\scriptsize, gray, anchor=west] at (4,3.5) {possible edges};
            \draw[gray, thin, dashed] (3.7,3.5) -- (3.95,3.5);

            \node[cross out, draw=red!70!black, thick, inner sep=2pt] at (-0.35,0.3) {};
            \node[cross out, draw=red!70!black, thick, inner sep=2pt] at (-0.35,1.0) {};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    It remains to prove the reverse inequality
    \[
        m(G)\ge |S|-\delta.
    \]

    Let $D$ be a set of $\delta$ new vertices disjoint from $S\cup T$, and define a
    new bipartite graph
    \[
        G^*=(S\cup (T\cup D),E^*),
    \]
    where
    \[
        E^*:=E\cup \{\{s,d\}: s\in S,\ d\in D\}.
    \]
    In other words, each vertex of $D$ is joined to every vertex of $S$.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                yscale=0.95, xscale=1.0,

                tvert/.style={vertex, fill=black!40, minimum size=5pt, label distance=2pt},

            ]
            % --- Left: G* with matching M* ---

                \draw[gray, thin, rounded corners] (2.5,2.4) rectangle (3.5,5.3);

    \end{center}

    Now let $A\subseteq S$. In $G^*$, the neighborhood of $A$ is
    \[
        N^*(A)=N(A)\cup D.
    \]
    Since $D\cap T=\varnothing$, this union is disjoint, so
    \[
        |N^*(A)|=|N(A)|+|D|=|N(A)|+\delta.
    \]
    By the definition of $\delta$ as the maximum of $|A|-|N(A)|$, we have
    \[
        |A|-|N(A)|\le \delta
    \]
    for every $A\subseteq S$. Rearranging gives
    \[
        |A|\le |N(A)|+\delta=|N^*(A)|.
    \]
    Thus Hall's condition holds in $G^*$:
    \[
        |A|\le |N^*(A)|\qquad\text{for all }A\subseteq S.
    \]

    Therefore, by Hall's theorem, there exists a matching $M^*$ in $G^*$ which
    saturates $S$. In particular,
    \[
        |M^*|=|S|.
    \]

    Some edges of $M^*$ may use vertices of $D$. Since each vertex of $D$ can be
    incident with at most one edge of the matching, the number of such edges is at
    most
    \[
        |D|=\delta.
    \]
    Delete from $M^*$ all edges incident with $D$. The remaining edges still form a
    matching, and all of them belong to the original graph $G$, because the only
    edges added when passing from $G$ to $G^*$ were those incident with $D$.

    Hence we obtain a matching in $G$ of size at least
    \[
        |S|-\delta.
    \]
    So
    \[
        m(G)\ge |S|-\delta.
    \]

    Combining the two inequalities yields
    \[
        m(G)=|S|-\delta
        =|S|-\max_{A\subseteq S}\bigl(|A|-|N(A)|\bigr).
    \]
    This proves the proposition.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[K\H{o}nig, 1931]\label{thm:konig}
    In a bipartite graph $G=(S\cup T,E)$, we have
    \[
        \max\{|M|:M\text{ matching}\}=\min\{|C|:C\text{ vertex cover}\}.
    \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    By Proposition~\ref{prop:deficiency}, $m(G)=|S|-|A_0|+|N(A_0)|=|S\setminus A_0|+|N(A_0)|$ for some $A_0$.
    Note that $N(A_0)\cup (S\setminus A_0)$ is a vertex cover.
    So $\min|C|\leqslant \max|M|$.

    On the other hand, $|C|\geqslant |M|$ for any vertex cover $C$ and any matching $M$, because $C$ must cover each edge of $M$.
    Therefore, $\min|C|=\max|M|$.
\end{proof}

\section{Transversal Theory}

There is a one-to-one correspondence between bipartite graphs and families of subsets of $[n]$, where $n=|S|$.

\begin{definition}
    Let $\mathcal{F}$ be a family of subsets of $[n]$.
    A \textbf{transversal} of $\mathcal{F}$ is an injective function $\varphi:\mathcal{F}\to[n]$ such that $\varphi(F)\in F$ for each $F\in\mathcal{F}$.
\end{definition}

The following theorem is equivalent to Theorem~\ref{thm:hall}.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:transversal}
    Let $\mathcal{F}=\{F_1,\ldots,F_m\}$ be a family of subsets of $[n]$.
    Then $\mathcal{F}$ has a transversal if and only if $\bigl|\bigcup_{i\in I}F_i\bigr|\geqslant |I|$ for all $I\subseteq[m]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    We show that the theorem is equivalent to Hall's Marriage Theorem.

    Given the family
    \[
        \mathcal F=\{F_1,\dots,F_m\},
    \]
    construct a bipartite graph
    \[
        G=(S\cup T,E)
    \]
    as follows:
    \[
        S=\{1,2,\dots,m\},\qquad T=[n].
    \]
    For each $i\in S$ and $j\in T$, put
    \[
        \{i,j\}\in E \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad j\in F_i.
    \]
    Thus the neighbors of $i\in S$ are exactly the elements of $F_i$.

    We now compare the two statements.

    \medskip

    \noindent
    \textbf{Transversals correspond to matchings saturating $S$.}

    Suppose first that $\mathcal F$ has a transversal, i.e. an injective map
    \[
        \varphi:\mathcal F\to [n]
    \]
    such that $\varphi(F_i)\in F_i$ for every $i$.

    For each $i\in S$, join $i$ to the vertex $\varphi(F_i)\in T$.
    Since $\varphi(F_i)\in F_i$, this is an edge of $G$.
    Since $\varphi$ is injective, these chosen vertices of $T$ are all distinct.
    Hence these edges form a matching saturating $S$.

    Conversely, suppose that $G$ has a matching saturating $S$.
    Then for each $i\in S$ there is a unique vertex $j_i\in T$ such that
    $i$ is matched to $j_i$.
    Because $\{i,j_i\}\in E$, we have $j_i\in F_i$.
    Since the matching uses distinct vertices of $T$, the map
    \[
        F_i\longmapsto j_i
    \]
    is injective. Therefore it is a transversal of $\mathcal F$.

    So $\mathcal F$ has a transversal if and only if $G$ has a matching
    saturating $S$.

    \medskip

    \noindent
    \textbf{The union condition corresponds to Hall's condition.}

    Let $I\subseteq [m]$, and regard $I$ as a subset of $S$.
    Then the neighborhood of $I$ in $G$ is
    \[
        N(I)=\bigcup_{i\in I}F_i.
    \]
    Indeed, a vertex $j\in T$ is adjacent to some $i\in I$ exactly when
    $j\in F_i$ for some $i\in I$.

    Therefore the condition
    \[
        \left|\bigcup_{i\in I}F_i\right|\ge |I|
        \qquad\text{for all }I\subseteq [m]
    \]
    is exactly Hall's condition
    \[
        |N(A)|\ge |A|
        \qquad\text{for all }A\subseteq S.
    \]

    \medskip

    By Hall's Marriage Theorem, $G$ has a matching saturating $S$ if and only if
    \[
        |N(A)|\ge |A|\qquad\text{for all }A\subseteq S.
    \]
    By the two correspondences above, this is equivalent to saying that
    $\mathcal F$ has a transversal if and only if
    \[
        \left|\bigcup_{i\in I}F_i\right|\ge |I|
        \qquad\text{for all }I\subseteq [m].
    \]
    Hence the theorem is equivalent to Hall's Marriage Theorem.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Birkhoff, 1946]
    Let $A=(a_{ij})\in M_n(\mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0})$ be an $n\times n$ matrix with nonnegative
    integer entries such that every row sum and every column sum equals $\ell$.
    Then $A$ can be written as the sum of $\ell$ permutation matrices.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    We argue by induction on $\ell$.

    If $\ell=0$, then $A=0$, so the statement is trivial.
    If $\ell=1$, then each row contains exactly one entry equal to $1$ and all other
    entries are $0$, and the same holds for each column. Hence $A$ itself is a
    permutation matrix.

    Now assume $\ell\ge 1$ and that the result holds for $\ell-1$.
    Let
    \[
        F_i:=\{\,j\in [n]: a_{ij}>0\,\},\qquad 1\le i\le n.
    \]
    We claim that the family $\mathcal F=\{F_1,\dots,F_n\}$ satisfies Hall's condition:
    for every subset $I\subseteq [n]$,
    \[
        \left|\bigcup_{i\in I}F_i\right|\ge |I|.
    \]

    Indeed, fix $I\subseteq [n]$ and write $k:=|I|$.
    Consider the rows indexed by $I$.
    Since each of these rows has sum $\ell$, the total sum of all entries in these rows is
    \[
        \sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j=1}^n a_{ij}=k\ell.
    \]
    Let
    \[
        J:=\bigcup_{i\in I}F_i.
    \]
    By definition of $F_i$, the entries of the rows indexed by $I$ are zero outside the
    columns in $J$. Hence all of the total weight $k\ell$ from those rows is concentrated
    in the columns of $J$.

    On the other hand, each column of $A$ has sum $\ell$, so any single column can
    contribute at most $\ell$ to the sum of the entries in the rows indexed by $I$.
    Therefore
    \[
        k\ell \le |J|\ell.
    \]
    Since $\ell>0$, dividing by $\ell$ gives
    \[
        |J|=\left|\bigcup_{i\in I}F_i\right|\ge k=|I|.
    \]
    This proves Hall's condition.

    By the transversal theorem, there exists an injective map
    \[
        \varphi:\{F_1,\dots,F_n\}\to [n]
    \]
    such that $\varphi(F_i)\in F_i$ for every $i$.
    Equivalently, for each $i\in [n]$ we may choose a distinct column
    \[
        j_i:=\varphi(F_i)\in F_i,
    \]
    so that $a_{i j_i}>0$ and the numbers $j_1,\dots,j_n$ are pairwise distinct.

    Now define an $n\times n$ matrix $P=(p_{ij})$ by
    \[
        p_{ij}=
        \begin{cases}
            1, & j=j_i,    \\
            0, & j\ne j_i.
        \end{cases}
    \]
    Because the columns $j_1,\dots,j_n$ are all distinct, each row of $P$ contains exactly
    one $1$, and each column of $P$ contains exactly one $1$. Thus $P$ is a permutation
    matrix.

    Moreover, whenever $p_{ij}=1$, we have $j=j_i\in F_i$, so $a_{ij}>0$.
    Hence every entry of $A-P$ is still a nonnegative integer.
    Also, each row sum of $A-P$ equals $\ell-1$, since we subtract exactly one $1$ from
    each row, and similarly each column sum of $A-P$ equals $\ell-1$.

    Therefore $A-P$ is a nonnegative integer matrix all of whose row sums and column
    sums are $\ell-1$. By the induction hypothesis, $A-P$ is a sum of $\ell-1$
    permutation matrices. Adding back $P$, we conclude that $A$ is a sum of $\ell$
    permutation matrices.
\end{proof}

We can identify a bipartite graph with an $s$-$t$ flow network by adding a source $s$ connected to every vertex in $S$ and a sink $t$ connected to every vertex in $T$, each edge having capacity $1$.

            tvert/.style={vertex, fill=black!40, minimum size=5pt},

        ]

        \draw[thick] (s1) -- (t1);
        \draw[thick] (s1) -- (t2);
        \draw[thick] (s2) -- (t2);
        \draw[thick] (s2) -- (t3);
        \draw[thick] (s3) -- (t1);
        \draw[thick] (s3) -- (t3);
        \draw[thick] (s4) -- (t3);
        \draw[thick] (s4) -- (t4);

        \draw[->, thick, red!60!black] (t\j) -- (t) node[caplabel, above, sloped] {$1$};

        \draw[gray, thin, rounded corners] (-0.5, -0.1) rectangle (0.5, 4.5);

    \end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{definition}[$s$-$t$ flow]
    Let $N=(V,E)$ be a directed network with source $s\in V$, sink $t\in V$, and
    capacity function
    \[
        c:E\to \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}.
    \]
    An \emph{$s$-$t$ flow} is a function
    \[
        f:E\to \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}
    \]
    such that:

    \begin{enumerate}
        \item[(i)] \textbf{Capacity constraint:}
              \[
                  0\le f(e)\le c(e)\qquad \text{for all } e\in E.
              \]

        \item[(ii)] \textbf{Flow conservation:} for every vertex $v\in V\setminus\{s,t\}$,
              \[
                  \sum_{\substack{(u,v)\in E}} f(u,v)
                  \;=\;
                  \sum_{\substack{(v,w)\in E}} f(v,w).
              \]
    \end{enumerate}

    The \emph{value} of the flow $f$ is defined by
    \[
        |f|
        :=
        \sum_{\substack{(s,v)\in E}} f(s,v)
    \]
    Equivalently, by flow conservation,
    \[
        |f|
        =
        \sum_{\substack{(u,t)\in E}} f(u,t)
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[$s$-$t$ cut]
    Let $N=(V,E)$ be a directed network with source $s$ and sink $t$.
    An \emph{$s$-$t$ cut} is a partition $(S,T)$ of $V$ such that
    \[
        S\cup T=V,\qquad S\cap T=\varnothing,\qquad s\in S,\qquad t\in T.
    \]
    The \emph{capacity} of the cut $(S,T)$ is
    \[
        c(S,T):=\sum_{\substack{(u,v)\in E\\ u\in S,\ v\in T}} c(u,v).
    \]
    That is, one sums the capacities of all directed edges going from $S$ to $T$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Max-flow min-cut theorem]\label{thm:maxflow}
    The maximum value of an $s$-$t$ flow is equal to the minimum capacity over all $s$-$t$ cuts.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    We divide the proof into two parts.

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{Step 1: For every flow $f$ and every $s$-$t$ cut $(S,T)$, one has}
    \[
        |f|\le c(S,T).
    \]

    Indeed, let $(S,T)$ be an $s$-$t$ cut, so $s\in S$ and $t\in T$.

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                xscale=1.6, yscale=1.2, >=stealth,
                fwd/.style={->, thick},
                elabel/.style={font=\scriptsize, midway, auto},
            ]
            \fill[blue!8, rounded corners=8pt] (-0.6, -0.5) rectangle (2.1, 3.3);
            \fill[red!8, rounded corners=8pt] (3.4, -0.5) rectangle (6.1, 3.3);
            \node[font=\footnotesize, blue!70!black] at (0.75, 3.6) {$S$};
            \node[font=\footnotesize, red!70!black] at (4.75, 3.6) {$T$};

            \node[vertex, fill=blue!60, minimum size=7pt, label={[font=\small]left:$s$}] (s) at (0, 1.4) {};
            \node[vertex, minimum size=5pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]above:$a$}] (a) at (1.5, 2.6) {};
            \node[vertex, minimum size=5pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]below:$b$}] (b) at (1.5, 0.2) {};
            \node[vertex, minimum size=5pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]above:$c$}] (c) at (4, 2.6) {};
            \node[vertex, minimum size=5pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]below:$d$}] (d) at (4, 0.2) {};
            \node[vertex, fill=red!60, minimum size=7pt, label={[font=\small]right:$t$}] (t) at (5.5, 1.4) {};

            \draw[fwd, blue!70!black] (s) -- (a) node[elabel] {$3/5$};
            \draw[fwd, blue!70!black] (s) -- (b) node[elabel, swap] {$2/3$};
            \draw[fwd, gray!70] (a) -- (b) node[elabel] {$1/2$};
            \draw[fwd, red!70!black, very thick] (a) -- (c) node[elabel] {$\mathbf{2/4}$};
            \draw[fwd, red!70!black, very thick] (b) -- (d) node[elabel, swap] {$\mathbf{3/3}$};
            \draw[fwd] (c) -- (t) node[elabel] {$2/4$};
            \draw[fwd] (d) -- (t) node[elabel, swap] {$3/3$};

            \draw[thick, dashed, red!60!black]
            (2.75, 3.5) -- (2.75, -0.7)
            node[below, font=\scriptsize, red!60!black] {cut $(S,T)$};

            \node[font=\scriptsize, align=left, text=red!60!black] at (2.75, -1.3)
            {$c(S,T) = 4 + 3 = 7$};
            \node[font=\scriptsize, align=left, text=blue!60!black] at (2.75, -1.8)
            {$|f| = 3+2 = 5 \le 7$};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    Consider the net flow leaving $S$:
    \[
        \sum_{\substack{u\in S,\ v\in T}} f(u,v)
        \;-\;
        \sum_{\substack{u\in T,\ v\in S}} f(u,v).
    \]
    By flow conservation at every vertex in $S\setminus\{s\}$, all internal contributions
    inside $S$ cancel, and this net flow is exactly the value of the flow:
    \[
        |f|
        =
        \sum_{\substack{u\in S,\ v\in T}} f(u,v)
        -
        \sum_{\substack{u\in T,\ v\in S}} f(u,v).
    \]
    Since $f(u,v)\ge 0$ for all edges, we obtain
    \[
        |f|
        \le
        \sum_{\substack{u\in S,\ v\in T}} f(u,v).
    \]
    By the capacity constraint $f(u,v)\le c(u,v)$,
    \[
        |f|
        \le
        \sum_{\substack{u\in S,\ v\in T}} c(u,v)
        =
        c(S,T).
    \]
    Thus every $s$-$t$ flow has value at most the capacity of every $s$-$t$ cut.
    Consequently,
    \[
        \max\{|f|: f \text{ is an $s$-$t$ flow}\}
        \le
        \min\{c(S,T): (S,T)\text{ is an $s$-$t$ cut}\}.
    \]

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{Step 2: Construct a flow whose value equals the capacity of some cut.}

    Let $f$ be a flow of maximum value.
    Consider the residual network $N_f$, whose vertex set is $V$ and whose edges are
    defined as follows:

    \begin{itemize}
        \item if $(u,v)\in E$ and $f(u,v)<c(u,v)$, then there is a \emph{forward} residual
              edge $u\to v$ with residual capacity $c(u,v)-f(u,v)$;
        \item if $(u,v)\in E$ and $f(u,v)>0$, then there is a \emph{backward} residual
              edge $v\to u$ with residual capacity $f(u,v)$.
    \end{itemize}

    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[
                xscale=1.1, yscale=1.0, >=stealth,
                fwd/.style={->, thick},
                rfwd/.style={->, thick, densely dashed, blue!70!black},
                rback/.style={->, thick, densely dashed, red!70!black},
                elabel/.style={font=\scriptsize, midway, fill=white, inner sep=1pt},
                vertex/.style={circle, fill=black, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=5pt}
            ]

            %---------------- Original network ----------------%
            \begin{scope}[local bounding box=orig]
                \node[font=\small\bfseries] at (1.2, 3.3) {Original network};

                \node[vertex, fill=blue!60, minimum size=6pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]left:$s$}] (s) at (0, 1.5) {};
                \node[vertex, label={[font=\scriptsize]above:$u$}] (u) at (1.2, 2.6) {};
                \node[vertex, label={[font=\scriptsize]below:$v$}] (v) at (1.2, 0.4) {};
                \node[vertex, fill=red!60, minimum size=6pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]right:$t$}] (t) at (2.4, 1.5) {};

                \draw[fwd] (s) -- (u) node[elabel, above left] {$3/3$};
                \draw[fwd] (s) -- (v) node[elabel, below left] {$2/2$};
                \draw[fwd] (u) -- (v) node[elabel, right] {$1/2$};
                \draw[fwd] (u) -- (t) node[elabel, above right] {$2/2$};
                \draw[fwd] (v) -- (t) node[elabel, below right] {$3/3$};

                \node[font=\scriptsize, blue!70!black] at (1.2, -0.3) {$|f|=4$};
            \end{scope}

            % arrow between pictures
            \node[font=\Large] at (3.7, 1.5) {$\Longrightarrow$};

            %---------------- Residual network ----------------%
            \begin{scope}[xshift=5.0cm, local bounding box=resid]
                \node[font=\small\bfseries] at (1.2, 3.3) {Residual network $N_f$};

                \node[vertex, fill=blue!60, minimum size=6pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]left:$s$}] (sr) at (0, 1.5) {};
                \node[vertex, label={[font=\scriptsize]above:$u$}] (ur) at (1.2, 2.6) {};
                \node[vertex, label={[font=\scriptsize]below:$v$}] (vr) at (1.2, 0.4) {};
                \node[vertex, fill=red!60, minimum size=6pt, label={[font=\scriptsize]right:$t$}] (tr) at (2.4, 1.5) {};

                % s <-> u
                \draw[rback] (ur) to[bend left=18] node[elabel, below right, text=red!70!black] {$3$} (sr);

                % v -> s
                \draw[rback] (vr) -- node[elabel, below left, text=red!70!black] {$2$} (sr);

                % u <-> v
                \draw[rfwd]  (ur) to[bend left=18] node[elabel, right, text=blue!70!black] {$1$} (vr);
                \draw[rback] (vr) to[bend left=18] node[elabel, left, text=red!70!black] {$1$} (ur);

                % u <-> t
                \draw[rback] (tr) to[bend left=18] node[elabel, below left, text=red!70!black] {$2$} (ur);

                % t -> v
                \draw[rback] (tr) -- node[elabel, below right, text=red!70!black] {$3$} (vr);
            \end{scope}

            % legend
            \node[font=\scriptsize, align=center] at (3.7, -0.35) {%
                \textcolor{blue!70!black}{blue dashed: forward residual edge}\\
                \textcolor{red!70!black}{red dashed: backward residual edge}
            };

        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    Since $f$ has maximum value, there is no $s$-$t$ path in the residual network.
    For otherwise, one could augment the flow along such a path and obtain a larger flow,
    contrary to the maximality of $f$.

    Now let $S$ be the set of vertices reachable from $s$ in the residual network,
    and let
    \[
        T:=V\setminus S.
    \]
    Then $s\in S$. Also $t\notin S$, since there is no residual $s$-$t$ path.
    Hence $(S,T)$ is an $s$-$t$ cut.

    We now examine the edges crossing this cut.

    \smallskip
    \noindent
    \emph{Claim 1.} If $u\in S$ and $v\in T$, then $f(u,v)=c(u,v)$.

    Indeed, if $f(u,v)<c(u,v)$, then the forward residual edge $u\to v$ would exist.
    Since $u$ is reachable from $s$, this would make $v$ reachable from $s$ as well,
    contradicting $v\in T$.

    \smallskip
    \noindent
    \emph{Claim 2.} If $u\in T$ and $v\in S$, then $f(u,v)=0$.

    Indeed, if $f(u,v)>0$, then the backward residual edge $v\to u$ would exist.
    Since $v$ is reachable from $s$, this would imply that $u$ is also reachable from
    $s$, contradicting $u\in T$.

    \smallskip
    Now compute the value of the flow using the cut $(S,T)$:
    \[
        |f|
        =
        \sum_{\substack{u\in S,\ v\in T}} f(u,v)
        -
        \sum_{\substack{u\in T,\ v\in S}} f(u,v).
    \]
    By Claims 1 and 2, this becomes
    \[
        |f|
        =
        \sum_{\substack{u\in S,\ v\in T}} c(u,v)-0
        =
        c(S,T).
    \]
    Thus the value of the maximum flow equals the capacity of the cut $(S,T)$.

    Combining this with Step 1, we conclude that
    \[
        \max\{|f|: f \text{ is an $s$-$t$ flow}\}
        =
        \min\{c(S,T): (S,T)\text{ is an $s$-$t$ cut}\}.
    \]
    This proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    For a bipartite graph $G=(A\cup B,E)$, form the standard flow network by adding a source $s$, a sink $t$, edges $s\to A$, $A\to B$, and $B\to t$, all of capacity $1$. Then integral flows correspond exactly to matchings, while $s$-$t$ cuts correspond to vertex covers. Thus the max-flow min-cut theorem is equivalent, in this setting, to K\"onig's theorem:
    \[
        \text{maximum matching size}=\text{minimum vertex cover size}.
    \]
    This also gives an efficient algorithm for computing a maximum matching in a bipartite graph.
\end{remark}

\chapter{Lecture 6}

\section{Chains and Antichains}

\begin{definition}
    A \textbf{partially ordered set} (\textbf{poset}) is a set $P$ together with a binary relation $<$ which is transitive and antisymmetric, i.e.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item if $x<y$ and $y<z$, then $x<z$;
        \item if $x<y$, then $y \nless x$.
    \end{itemize}
    Write $x\leqslant y$ if $x<y$ or $x=y$.

    Elements $x,y\in P$ are \textbf{comparable} if $x\leqslant y$ or $y\leqslant x$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    A \textbf{chain} $C$ is a subset of $P$ such that all its elements are pairwise comparable.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    An \textbf{antichain} $A$ is a subset of $P$ such that any two distinct elements of $A$ are incomparable.
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Questions.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item How many chains are needed to partition $P$?
    \item How many antichains are needed to partition $P$?
\end{itemize}

\begin{theorem}[Mirsky]
    Suppose that the longest chain in a poset $P$ has size $r$. Then $P$ can be partitioned into $r$ antichains (Obviously it can not be partitioned in to fewer than $r$ antichains).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let $A_i$ be the set of $x\in P$ such that the longest chain whose greatest element is $x$ has size $i$.
    Then $A_i=\emptyset$ for $i>r$.
    Thus $P=A_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup A_r$ is a partition of $P$.
    For instance, in the poset below one has $r=3$, with $b\in A_1$, $\{m_1,m_2\}\subseteq A_2$, and $t\in A_3$ (the shaded strips indicate the antichains $A_i$).
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.05, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            % horizontal layers for A_1, A_2, A_3
            \fill[ThmBg!70] (-2.1,-0.05) rectangle (2.6,0.88);
            \fill[LemBg!55] (-2.1,0.92) rectangle (2.6,1.88);
            \fill[ExBg!90] (-2.1,1.92) rectangle (2.6,2.85);
            \node[left, font=\footnotesize] at (-2.05,0.42) {$A_1$};
            \node[left, font=\footnotesize] at (-2.05,1.40) {$A_2$};
            \node[left, font=\footnotesize] at (-2.05,2.38) {$A_3$};
            % Hasse diagram: b < m1,m2 < t
            \node[vertex, label=below:$b$] (Mb) at (0.2,0.35) {};
            \node[vertex, label=left:$m_1$] (Mm1) at (-0.85,1.40) {};
            \node[vertex, label=right:$m_2$] (Mm2) at (1.25,1.40) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:$t$] (Mt) at (0.2,2.38) {};
            \draw (Mb) -- (Mm1) -- (Mt) -- (Mm2) -- (Mb);
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}
    Furthermore, each $A_i$ is an antichain.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Dilworth's theorem]\label{thm:dilworth}
    Suppose that the longest antichain in a poset $P$ has size $r$. Then $P$ can be partitioned into $r$ chains.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    We argue by induction on $|P|$.
    The case $|P|=1$ is trivial.

    Let $a$ be a maximal element of $P$.
    Let $r$ be the size of the longest antichain in $P\setminus\{a\}=:P'$.
    By the induction hypothesis, $P'$ is the union of $r$ pairwise disjoint chains $C_1,\dots,C_r$.
    The next picture is only schematic ($r=3$): each $C_i$ is a chain in $P'$, and the highlighted vertices will later play the role of the elements $a_i$.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            \node[vertex] (Dp10) at (-2.4,0) {};
            \node[vertex] (Dp11) at (-2.4,1.0) {};
            \node[vertex, fill=ThmColor!75, draw=ThmColor, line width=0.35pt, label=left:$a_1$] (Dp12) at (-2.4,2.1) {};
            \draw (Dp10)--(Dp11)--(Dp12);
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (-2.4,-0.28) {$C_1$};
            \node[vertex] (Dp20) at (0,0) {};
            \node[vertex, fill=ThmColor!75, draw=ThmColor, line width=0.35pt, label=right:$a_2$] (Dp21) at (0,1.35) {};
            \draw (Dp20)--(Dp21);
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (0,-0.28) {$C_2$};
            \node[vertex] (Dp30) at (2.4,0) {};
            \node[vertex] (Dp31) at (2.4,1.0) {};
            \node[vertex, fill=ThmColor!75, draw=ThmColor, line width=0.35pt, label=right:$a_3$] (Dp32) at (2.4,2.0) {};
            \draw (Dp30)--(Dp31)--(Dp32);
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (2.4,-0.28) {$C_3$};
            \node[font=\footnotesize, align=center] at (0,-1.5) {Every $r$-element antichain in $P$ meets each $C_i$ in one point;\\[-1pt]the maxima $a_i$ chosen there form an antichain $A=\{a_1,\dots,a_r\}$.};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    We aim to show that either
    \begin{itemize}
        \item the longest antichain in $P$ has size $r+1$, or
        \item $P$ is a union of $r$ chains.
    \end{itemize}

    Every $r$-element antichain of $P$ contains one element of each $C_i$.
    Let $a_i$ denote the largest element of $C_i$ that belongs to such an antichain (in the chain order).
    Then $A=\{a_1,\dots,a_r\}$ is an antichain in $P$.
    If $A\cup\{a\}$ is an antichain, then we are done (adjoin $\{a\}=C_{r+1}$).
    Otherwise $a>a_i$ for some $i$, and then
    \[
        K=\{a\}\cup\{x\in C_i:x\leqslant a_i\}
    \]
    is a chain in $P$.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            \node[vertex] (Dq10) at (-2.4,0) {};
            \node[vertex] (Dq11) at (-2.4,1.0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=left:$a_1$] (Dq12) at (-2.4,2.1) {};
            \draw (Dq10)--(Dq11)--(Dq12);
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (-2.4,-0.28) {$C_1$};
            \node[vertex] (Dq20) at (0,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=left:$a_2$] (Dq21) at (0,1.35) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:$a$] (Dqa) at (0,2.65) {};
            \draw[very thick, ThmColor] (Dq20)--(Dq21)--(Dqa);
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (0,-0.28) {$C_2$};
            \node[font=\footnotesize, ThmColor] at (0.95,1.15) {$K$};
            \node[vertex] (Dq30) at (2.4,0) {};
            \node[vertex] (Dq31) at (2.4,1.0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=right:$a_3$] (Dq32) at (2.4,2.0) {};
            \draw (Dq30)--(Dq31)--(Dq32);
            \node[below, font=\footnotesize] at (2.4,-0.28) {$C_3$};
            \node[font=\footnotesize, align=center] at (0,-1.5) {Illustration for $i=2$: if $a>a_2$, then $K$ is the lower part of $C_2$ together with $a$.};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    We claim that there is no $r$-element antichain in $P\setminus K$.
    Otherwise, there exists an antichain $A'$ of size $r$ contained in $P\setminus K$, and there exists $a_i'\in A'\cap C_i$.
    This implies that $a_i'\leqslant a_i$ and hence $a_i'\in K$, a contradiction.

    By the induction hypothesis, $P\setminus K$ can be partitioned into $r-1$ chains.
    Together with $K$, this partitions $P$ into $r$ chains.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    There is another proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:hall} (Hall's Marriage Theorem) along these lines.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose that $S_1,\dots,S_m$ satisfy $|I|\leqslant |S(I)|$ for all $I\subseteq [m]$, where
    $S(I)=\bigcup_{i\in I} S_i$.

    Define a poset $P$ as follows:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{Points:} elements of $\bigcup_{i=1}^m S_i$ and symbols $y_1,\dots,y_m$.
        \item \textbf{Order:} declare $x<y_i$ if $x\in S_i$.
    \end{itemize}

    Clearly, $X:=\bigcup_{i=1}^m S_i$ is an antichain in $P$.
    Moreover, it is a longest antichain: let $A$ be an antichain and put $I:=\{i:y_i\in A\}$.
    Then $A$ contains no element of $S(I)$.
    Hence
    \[
        |A|\leqslant |I|+|X|-|S(I)|\leqslant |X|.
    \]

    By Theorem~\ref{thm:dilworth}, the set $P$ can be partitioned into $|X|$ chains.
    By the pigeonhole principle, there are $m$ chains of length $2$.
    In the disjoint example, each chain of length $2$ is $\{x_i,y_i\}$; these edges form a matching between $\{y_1,y_2,y_3\}$ and $\{x_1,x_2,x_3\}$.
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95, every node/.style={font=\small}]
            \node[vertex, label=below:$x_1$] (H2x1) at (-1.7,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:$x_2$] (H2x2) at (0,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=below:$x_3$] (H2x3) at (1.7,0) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:$y_1$] (H2y1) at (-1.7,2) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:$y_2$] (H2y2) at (0,2) {};
            \node[vertex, label=above:$y_3$] (H2y3) at (1.7,2) {};
            \draw[very thick, ThmColor] (H2x1)--(H2y1);
            \draw[very thick, ThmColor] (H2x2)--(H2y2);
            \draw[very thick, ThmColor] (H2x3)--(H2y3);
            \node[font=\footnotesize, align=center] at (0,-0.72) {The $m$ two-element chains (bold) are the edges of a matching in the bipartite graph $(S\cup T,E)$.};
        \end{tikzpicture}
    \end{center}

    Let $S=\{y_1,\dots,y_m\}$ and $T=\bigcup_{i=1}^m S_i$.
    Then these $m$ chains correspond to a maximum matching in the bipartite graph $(S\cup T,E)$.
\end{proof}

\section{Hadamard Matrices}

\begin{definition}
    A \textbf{Hadamard matrix} of order $n$ is an $(n\times n)$ matrix $H$ with entries in $\{-1,1\}$ such that
    \[
        HH^{\mathsf T}=nI.
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}\leavevmode
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The $1\times 1$ matrix $H=(1), H=(-1)$ and $H=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix}, H=\begin{pmatrix}-1&-1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix}$ are examples of Hadamard matrices of orders $1$ and $2$.
        \item There exists no Hadamard matrix of order $3$.
    \end{itemize}
\end{example}

Let $H$ be a Hadamard matrix.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Multiplying a row or a column of $H$ by $-1$ yields a Hadamard matrix.
    \item Permuting rows or columns of $H$ yields a Hadamard matrix.
\end{itemize}
Hence, after these operations we may assume that $H$ has the form
\[
    \begin{bmatrix}
        1      & 1 & \cdots        & 1 \\
        1      &   &               &   \\
        \vdots &   & \widetilde{H} &   \\
        1      &   &               &
    \end{bmatrix},
\]
where the first row and first column consist entirely of $1$s (and $\widetilde{H}$ is an $(n-1)\times(n-1)$ block).

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:hadamard-order}
    If $H$ is a Hadamard matrix of order $n$, then $n=1$, $n=2$, or $n\equiv 0\pmod{4}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Recall that a Hadamard matrix of order $n$ is an $n\times n$ matrix
    with entries in $\{\pm1\}$ such that
    \[
        HH^{\mathsf T}=nI_n.
    \]
    Equivalently, any two distinct rows are orthogonal.

    We must show that if $n>2$, then $4\mid n$.

    By multiplying rows or columns by $-1$, and by permuting rows or columns,
    we obtain an equivalent Hadamard matrix. These operations preserve the
    Hadamard property, so without loss of generality we may assume that $H$
    is \emph{normalized}, i.e. its first row and first column consist entirely of $+1$'s.

    Now assume $n>2$.

    Since the first row is all $+1$'s and the second row is orthogonal to it, the sum of
    the entries in the second row must be $0$. Hence the second row contains equally many
    $+1$'s and $-1$'s. In particular, $n$ is even.

    Next consider the first three rows of $H$.
    After permuting columns if necessary, we may arrange that these three rows have the form
    \[
        \begin{array}{cccc}
            \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{a}
             & \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{b}
             & \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{c}
             & \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{d} \\[4pt]
            \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{a}
             & \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{b}
             & \underbrace{-\ \cdots\ -}_{c}
             & \underbrace{-\ \cdots\ -}_{d} \\[4pt]
            \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{a}
             & \underbrace{-\ \cdots\ -}_{b}
             & \underbrace{+\ \cdots\ +}_{c}
             & \underbrace{-\ \cdots\ -}_{d}
        \end{array}
    \]
    for some nonnegative integers $a,b,c,d$ satisfying
    \[
        a+b+c+d=n.
    \]

    In other words, we have partitioned the columns according to the sign patterns of
    the second and third rows relative to the first row.

    Now use orthogonality.

    \medskip
    \noindent
    \textbf{(1) Row 1 orthogonal to Row 2.}
    Their inner product is
    \[
        a+b-c-d=0.
    \]

    \noindent
    \textbf{(2) Row 1 orthogonal to Row 3.}
    Their inner product is
    \[
        a-b+c-d=0.
    \]

    \noindent
    \textbf{(3) Row 2 orthogonal to Row 3.}
    Their inner product is
    \[
        a-b-c+d=0.
    \]

    Thus we have the system
    \[
